“I created a transparent pricing section so customers can understand the trial offer and decide whether they are ready to pay.”

15:00–16:00 — Create the How-It-Works Section

During this session, I will explain the complete customer journey.

The section should make the booking process look simple without hiding important safety checks.

Section Heading

How PetSaathi works

Supporting Text

From submitting your requirement to receiving the final pet report, every step is clear and organised.

Step 1: Tell Us About Your Pet

The customer completes the booking form.

The form collects:

Pet type

Breed

Age

Behaviour

Required service

Location

Preferred date and time

Step 2: We Review the Requirement

The PetSaathi team checks:

Whether the area is supported

Whether the service is available

Whether the pet information is complete

Whether a suitable sitter is available

Whether additional safety information is required

Step 3: Receive a Suitable Match

The customer receives information about the proposed sitter.

The information may include:

First name

Experience

Approved services

Relevant verification badges

Availability

Trial price

Step 4: Confirm and Pay

The customer reviews:

Service details

Date and time

Sitter information

Price

Cancellation terms

Refund conditions

After reviewing the information, the customer completes the trial payment.

Step 5: Receive Updates

During the service, the customer receives the updates included in the booking.

These may include:

Arrival confirmation

Photographs

Location update

Walk status

Feeding update

Behaviour note

Step 6: Receive the Final Report

After service completion, the sitter submits the pet report card.

The customer can then:

Review the service

Submit a rating

Report a problem

Request another booking

How-It-Works Design

The process may be shown through:

Numbered cards

Connected timeline

Mobile vertical steps

Small icons

Short descriptions

The text should remain short enough to scan quickly.

Output

A six-step visual customer journey from booking request to final review.

Simple Explanation for Professor

“I created the How It Works section to show the complete customer journey in a simple step-by-step format.”

16:00–17:00 — Add Forms and WhatsApp Links

During this session, I will connect every important CTA to the correct conversion path.

This step converts the landing page from an information page into a validation tool.

Book-a-Trial Links

The following buttons should open the pet-parent form:

Book a Trial

Request a Trial Walk

Request Pet Sitting

Check Boarding Availability

Start Your Booking

The form should open either:

On a dedicated page

Inside a modal

Through an external form link

All buttons should lead to the same structured customer database.

Become-a-Sitter Links

The following buttons should open the sitter application:

Become a Sitter

Apply as a Sitter

Join the Caregiver Network

Society-Partnership Links

The following buttons should open the society form:

Partner Your Society

Request a Society Pilot

Organise a Pet-Care Camp

WhatsApp Button

A floating WhatsApp button should be available on the page.

The button should open a pre-filled message such as:

Hello, I am interested in the PetSaathi pilot. I need [dog walking/pet sitting/pet boarding] in [area].

The message should not contain sensitive pet, health or identity information.

Detailed information should be collected through the structured form or follow-up process.

Link-Tracking Requirements

Each important button should have a clear analytics name.

Examples include:

hero_book_trial_clicked

hero_sitter_apply_clicked

society_partner_clicked

dog_walk_trial_clicked

whatsapp_clicked

booking_form_opened

This will help the team understand which section produces the most customer actions.

Link Testing Checklist

I will verify that:

Every button opens the correct destination.

Form links work on desktop and mobile.

WhatsApp opens the correct number.

The pre-filled message appears correctly.

External links open safely.

No placeholder link remains.

Each CTA has an accessible label.

Back navigation works properly.

Output

A complete conversion flow connecting landing-page visitors to customer, sitter, society and WhatsApp actions.

Simple Explanation for Professor

“I connected every website button to the correct form or WhatsApp conversation so visitor interest can become a measurable lead.”

17:00–18:00 — Complete Mobile Responsiveness

During this session, I will test and improve the page for mobile, tablet and desktop screens.

Most validation visitors may open the page from a WhatsApp or Instagram link, so the mobile experience must be treated as a primary requirement.

Header Responsiveness

On smaller screens:

Hide the full desktop navigation.

Display a mobile-menu button.

Keep the PetSaathi logo visible.

Keep the main CTA accessible.

Prevent navigation items from overflowing.

Hero Responsiveness

On mobile:

Display the heading before the image.

Keep paragraphs short.

Stack CTA buttons vertically when required.

Use full-width tap-friendly buttons.

Reduce decorative elements.

Maintain clear spacing.

Service-Card Responsiveness

On desktop:

Display three cards in one row where space allows.

On tablet:

Display two cards per row when appropriate.

On mobile:

Display one card per row.

Each card should remain readable without horizontal scrolling.

Pricing Responsiveness

Pricing cards should:

Stack vertically on small screens.

Keep the price visually prominent.

Display all important conditions.

Avoid narrow text columns.

Keep CTA buttons easy to tap.

Trust and Process Responsiveness

The trust cards and process steps should move from a multi-column layout to a vertical layout on mobile devices.

The reading order should remain logical.

Typography Testing

I will check:

Heading sizes

Paragraph sizes

Line spacing

Button labels

Price readability

FAQ text

Footer links

Text should not appear too small on mobile screens.

Image Testing

I will ensure that:

Images maintain the correct proportion.

Images do not overflow their containers.

Large images are compressed or optimised.

Important content does not depend only on an image.

Alternative text is provided.

Image loading does not cause major layout movement.

Form-Link Testing

I will test the customer, sitter and society journeys on mobile devices.

The user should be able to:

Open the form

Enter information

Select dropdown options

Use date and time fields

Submit the form

Return to the landing page

Common Screen Sizes to Test

The page should be reviewed at approximately:

360-pixel mobile width

390-pixel mobile width

430-pixel mobile width

768-pixel tablet width

1024-pixel laptop width

1440-pixel desktop width

These are practical testing widths rather than guarantees for every possible device.

Mobile Quality Checklist

I will confirm that:

There is no horizontal scrolling.

Buttons are easy to tap.

Text is readable.

Images load correctly.

The navigation menu works.

Forms open correctly.

WhatsApp opens correctly.

Pricing is visible.

CTA buttons appear throughout the page.

Footer links are usable.

Sections have sufficient spacing.

The page does not contain broken content.

Output

A responsive PetSaathi landing page ready for desktop, tablet and mobile validation traffic.

Simple Explanation for Professor

“I tested and improved the complete landing page so it works properly on mobile phones, tablets and desktop screens.”

Finally, I tested and improved the complete website for mobile, tablet and desktop screens. The final output was a responsive landing page ready to collect real validation leads.”

PetSaathi Phase 1 — Day 3: Tracking and Launch Setup

Main Goal of Day 3

The main goal of Day 3 is to prepare the PetSaathi landing page for measurement, communication, promotion and early customer testing.

By the end of the day, I should have:

Google Analytics tracking

Microsoft Clarity heatmaps and recordings

Meta Pixel tracking

Fully tested customer, sitter and society forms

A complete WhatsApp Business profile

A professional Instagram profile

A shareable PetSaathi brochure

Feedback from the first ten test users

The website should not be publicly promoted until the forms, links, tracking and mobile experience have been tested properly.

09:00–10:00 — Add Google Analytics 4 Tracking

During this session, I will connect the PetSaathi landing page to Google Analytics 4, also called GA4.

GA4 will help me understand how visitors arrive on the website and what actions they perform.

Step 1: Create the Analytics Property

I will create a dedicated GA4 property for PetSaathi.

The property should use:

PetSaathi as the property name

The correct reporting time zone

The correct reporting currency

A separate web data stream

The final landing-page domain

The property should not be mixed with another personal or unrelated website.

Step 2: Add the GA4 Measurement ID

After creating the web data stream, I will receive a measurement ID.

The measurement ID normally follows a format similar to:

G-XXXXXXXXXX

I will add this ID to the Next.js project through an environment variable instead of writing it repeatedly inside different components.

Example variable name:

NEXT_PUBLIC_GA_MEASUREMENT_ID

This makes the configuration easier to maintain.

Step 3: Install the Website Tag

I will add the analytics tag to the main application layout so that it loads consistently across the landing page.

The tag should load efficiently and should not block the visible website content.

I will ensure that analytics is activated only according to the approved cookie and privacy configuration.

Step 4: Define Important GA4 Events

I will track important actions instead of measuring page views only.

General Website Events

page_view

scroll_depth_reached

faq_opened

pricing_viewed

Customer Events

book_trial_clicked

booking_form_opened

booking_form_started

booking_form_submitted

whatsapp_booking_clicked

Sitter Events

become_sitter_clicked

sitter_form_opened

sitter_form_submitted

Society Events

society_partner_clicked

society_form_opened

society_form_submitted

Payment Events

trial_payment_link_clicked

trial_payment_completed

Payment completion should be tracked only when the payment platform can provide a reliable confirmation.

Step 5: Add Useful Event Parameters

Where appropriate, an event may include non-sensitive information such as:

CTA location

Service type

City category

Landing-page section

Device category

Lead source

Campaign name

I will not send names, telephone numbers, email addresses, complete home addresses, pet medical information or exact GPS coordinates to GA4.

Step 6: Mark Important Events as Conversions

The most important actions should be treated as key conversions.

Suggested Phase 1 conversions include:

Booking form submitted

Sitter application submitted

Society form submitted

WhatsApp booking clicked

Trial payment completed

These events will show whether website visitors are taking useful business actions.

Step 7: Test Analytics

I will use GA4’s real-time or debugging tools to confirm that:

The website visit is recorded.

CTA clicks are recorded.

Form submissions are recorded.

WhatsApp clicks are recorded.

Events are not firing twice.

Personal information is not included.

Test activity is clearly identified.

Output

A working GA4 setup that measures page activity, lead-generation actions and trial conversions.

Simple Explanation for Professor

“I added Google Analytics so I can measure how many people visit PetSaathi, which buttons they click and how many people submit a form or request a trial.”

10:00–10:45 — Add Microsoft Clarity

During this session, I will connect Microsoft Clarity to the PetSaathi landing page.

Clarity will help me understand how visitors visually interact with the website.

Main Purpose

GA4 shows numerical data such as visits and conversions.

Clarity helps show behavioural patterns such as:

Where visitors click

How far visitors scroll

Which sections they ignore

Where users repeatedly click

Whether the mobile layout causes difficulty

Whether users leave before reaching the forms

Step 1: Create the Clarity Project

I will create a separate Clarity project using:

PetSaathi as the project name

The correct production domain

The relevant website category

Clarity will provide a project ID and installation script.

Step 2: Install the Tracking Script

I will add the Clarity script to the main application layout.

The script should be added once so it does not create duplicate recordings.

I will also ensure that it follows the required cookie-consent configuration.

Step 3: Review Sensitive Content

The landing page contains forms that may collect personal and pet information.

I will confirm that Clarity does not expose:

Customer names

Phone numbers

Email addresses

Service addresses

Medical details

Emergency contacts

Identity documents

Payment information

Sensitive form elements should remain masked.

If an embedded external form is used, I will verify how it appears inside Clarity recordings.

Step 4: Review Heatmaps

After test traffic is available, I will review:

Click Heatmap

This shows which buttons, links and page areas receive clicks.

Scroll Heatmap

This shows how far visitors move down the landing page.

Area Heatmap

This shows which sections receive greater attention.

Step 5: Review Session Recordings

I will use test recordings to identify problems such as:

Visitors cannot find the main CTA.

Users click an element that is not interactive.

The mobile menu is confusing.

A form link does not open.

The pricing section is missed.

A user leaves after seeing too much text.

The WhatsApp button covers important content.

Step 6: Record Findings

I will create a small UX issue sheet with the following columns:

Issue ID

Page section

Device

Observed behaviour

Possible cause

Priority

Proposed solution

Fix status

Output

A working Clarity setup with privacy-safe heatmaps and session recordings.

Simple Explanation for Professor

“I added Microsoft Clarity so I can visually understand how visitors use the landing page and identify confusing sections.”

10:45–11:30 — Add Meta Pixel

During this session, I will connect the website to Meta Pixel.

The main Phase 1 purpose is to measure visitors arriving from Instagram or Facebook and prepare for future retargeting campaigns.

Retargeting means showing a future advertisement to people who previously visited or interacted with the PetSaathi website.

Step 1: Prepare the Meta Business Setup

I will confirm that PetSaathi has:

A Meta Business portfolio

The correct Facebook Page, when used

The correct Instagram professional account

Access to Meta Events Manager

A website data source or Pixel

Step 2: Create or Select the Pixel

I will create a Pixel for PetSaathi and connect it to the correct website domain.

The Pixel ID should be stored in an environment variable.

Example:

NEXT_PUBLIC_META_PIXEL_ID

Step 3: Install the Base Pixel

I will add the Pixel base code to the main application layout.

The code should not be installed more than once.

The Pixel should load only according to the approved cookie-consent choice.

Step 4: Define Pixel Events

Suggested Phase 1 events include:

Page View

Triggered when the landing page loads.

View Content

Triggered when a visitor views an important section, such as services or pricing.

Lead

Triggered after a valid customer, sitter or society form submission.

Contact

Triggered when the visitor opens WhatsApp or another business-contact method.

Initiate Checkout

Triggered when a qualified customer opens a trial-payment link.

Purchase

Triggered only after confirmed successful payment.

The event names should be used consistently across the complete website.

Step 5: Avoid Sensitive Data

Meta Pixel events should not receive:

Pet medical details

Exact service addresses

Customer concerns written in text areas

Identity documents

Phone numbers

Email addresses

Emergency information

Payment credentials

Only the minimum approved event information should be included.

Step 6: Test the Pixel

I will use Meta Events Manager and its testing tools to confirm that:

The base Pixel loads.

The page-view event works.

CTA events work.

Lead events work.

Events fire only once.

No prohibited information is transmitted.

Events are associated with the correct website.

Output

A tested Meta Pixel setup ready to measure campaign actions and support future retargeting.

Simple Explanation for Professor

“I added Meta Pixel so I can measure leads coming from Instagram or Facebook and later reach interested visitors through advertising.”

11:30–12:30 — Test All Forms

During this session, I will test every customer journey from the landing page to the CRM.

Google Forms and similar form platforms allow forms to be published, shared, connected to response sheets and given customised confirmation messages. Every PetSaathi form should be tested through the actual responder link, not only through the editing screen.

Forms to Test

Pet-Parent Booking Form

This form collects customer, pet, location, service and trial-readiness information.

Sitter Application Form

This form collects caregiver experience, availability, requested services and verification readiness.

Society Partnership Form

This form collects society information, pet-family estimates and partnership interest.

Review Form

This form collects ratings and feedback after a completed trial.

Service Report Form

This form allows the sitter to submit the final service report.

Test 1: Required Fields

I will try submitting each form without completing required information.

The form should prevent incomplete submissions and display a clear message.

Test 2: Field Validation

I will check that:

Phone fields accept valid numbers.

Email fields use a valid format.

Age and weight fields reject invalid values.

Date fields do not accept impossible dates.

Time options are understandable.

Dropdown choices work.

Consent checkboxes function correctly.

Test 3: Conditional Questions

I will confirm that relevant questions appear only when required.

Examples include:

Boarding-home questions appear only for boarding applicants.

Medical-details questions appear only when relevant.

Marketing consent remains separate from service consent.

A “No” answer does not unnecessarily display follow-up fields.

Test 4: Mobile Completion

I will complete all forms on a mobile phone.

I will check:

Text fields

Dropdowns

Date selectors

Time selectors

Checkboxes

File uploads

Submit buttons

Confirmation screens

Test 5: CRM Connection

I will verify that every test response reaches the correct CRM sheet.

The data should appear under the correct columns.

For example:

Customer responses should not enter the sitter sheet.

Sitter applications should not enter the booking sheet.

Society leads should receive the correct lead type.

Dates and phone numbers should remain readable.

Test 6: Unique Record Identification

Every submitted record should receive or later be assigned a unique ID.

Examples include:

PP-001 for a pet-parent lead

ST-001 for a sitter applicant

SC-001 for a society lead

BK-001 for a booking

Test 7: Confirmation Message

After submission, the user should receive a clear confirmation.

Example:

“Thank you for submitting your PetSaathi trial request. Our team will review your information and contact you if your location and service match the current pilot.”

The message should not promise immediate approval.

Test 8: Duplicate Submission

I will check what happens when the same person submits the form more than once.

Duplicates should be identified through phone number, email address or another internal matching rule.

Test 9: Error Recovery

I will test:

Broken internet during submission

Incorrect file type

Oversized file

Missing permission

Expired form link

Closed form

Incorrect redirect

The user should receive a useful message instead of losing all information silently.

Test 10: Privacy Review

I will confirm that:

Sensitive responses are not publicly visible.

Response summaries are not shared with respondents.

Only authorised team members can open the sheets.

Identity files are not stored in public folders.

Test data is removed when it is no longer required.

Output

Fully tested forms with no broken links, missing responses or incorrect CRM mappings.

Simple Explanation for Professor

“I tested every form on desktop and mobile and confirmed that the submitted information reaches the correct tracking sheet.”

12:30–13:30 — Lunch Break

I will take a lunch break after completing analytics and form testing.

Before leaving, I will save:

GA4 test results

Clarity installation status

Meta Pixel test results

Form-testing checklist

List of remaining bugs

Output

No new project work is required during this period.

“I created the WhatsApp Business profile so PetSaathi can communicate professionally and organise different types of leads.”

Easy to remember

Consistent with the website

16:30–18:00 — Soft Launch to Ten People

During the final session, I will share the PetSaathi system with ten carefully selected test users.

This is a soft launch, not a full public launch.

The objective is to identify problems before sending the website to a larger audience.

Test Group Selection

The ten people should include a mixture of:

Pet parents

Potential sitters

Apartment residents

A society representative

A veterinarian or groomer

A web or design reviewer

A person unfamiliar with the project

Using different user types will produce more useful feedback.

What Each Tester Should Do

Pet-Parent Test

The tester should:

Open the landing page.

Explain what PetSaathi offers.

Find the services.

Find the trial price.

Click Book a Trial.

Complete the customer form.

Open WhatsApp.

Describe any confusing step.

Find the Become a Sitter section.

Open the application.

Review the required information.

Complete a test application.

Explain whether the verification process is clear.

Society Test

The tester should:

Find the society partnership section.

Understand the proposed partnership.

Open the society form.

Explain what additional information is required.

Feedback Questions

I will ask each tester:

What do you think PetSaathi offers?

Which section was easiest to understand?

Which section was confusing?

Could you find the trial price?

Could you find the booking button?

Observation Checklist

Device

Suggested improvement

Priority

Fix status

Issue Priority Levels

Critical

The user cannot complete the main action.

Examples:

Booking form does not open.

Form submission fails.

WhatsApp opens the wrong number.

Mobile layout hides the CTA.

High

The user can continue but important information is unclear.

Examples:

Prices are difficult to find.

Trust claims are confusing.

Pilot area is not visible.

Medium

The issue reduces clarity but does not block conversion.

Examples:

Paragraphs are too long.

Icons are unclear.

Spacing is inconsistent.

Low

The issue is mainly visual or optional.

Examples:

Small colour adjustment

Minor wording preference

Decorative alignment

Customer, sitter and society forms work.

WhatsApp opens correctly.

No critical mobile issue remains.

Analytics events are recorded.

Sensitive form information is protected.

Prices and pilot locations are visible.

Main trust questions are answered.

Critical and high-priority issues are fixed.

Structured feedback from ten users and a prioritised correction list.

I then created the WhatsApp Business profile, Instagram professional profile and digital brochure. Finally, I shared the system with ten test users, collected their feedback and corrected the major problems before the wider launch.”

PetSaathi Phase 1 — Day 4: Customer Outreach Batch 1

Main goal of Day 4

The main goal of Day 4 is to contact 20 suitable pet parents, understand their real pet-care problems and identify customers who may be willing to book a paid PetSaathi trial.

The objective is not to send messages to as many people as possible. The objective is to have useful conversations with pet parents who live in or near the selected launch areas.

Customer outreach should be permission-based. WhatsApp’s official business guidance states that businesses should secure a customer’s opt-in before initiating business messages. TRAI also defines consent as voluntary permission to receive calls or messages about a particular purpose, product or service. Therefore, PetSaathi should use referrals, society permission, form submissions and other consent-based sources instead of scraping telephone numbers or repeatedly messaging strangers.

Day 4 target

By the end of the day, I should have:

A list of at least 20 relevant pet-parent leads

Twenty outreach attempts

As many completed conversations as possible

Updated CRM records

Hot, warm and cold lead classifications

A list of the most common customer objections

Follow-up actions for interested customers

Clear evidence about trust, pricing and service demand

09:00–10:00 — Find Leads from Societies, Instagram, Dog Parks and Personal Contacts

During this session, I will prepare a list of potential pet parents who may be suitable for the PetSaathi validation sprint.

I will focus on people who live in the selected city and preferably within the two or three launch areas.

Source 1: Apartment Societies

I will contact apartment managers, resident-welfare association members, society committee members and known residents.

Instead of requesting a complete resident telephone list, I should ask the authorised contact person to:

Share the PetSaathi survey in the residents’ group

Introduce me to interested pet parents

Allow me to publish a short research message

Invite pet parents to voluntarily provide their contact details

Suitable society message

Hello, I am conducting research for PetSaathi, a proposed pet-care service for dog walking, pet sitting and controlled boarding.

We are looking to speak with pet parents in [Area] for a short 10-minute research conversation. Interested residents may contact us or complete the attached form. Participation is voluntary.

This method allows residents to decide whether they want to participate.

Source 2: Instagram

I will review relevant Instagram interactions, including:

People who replied to PetSaathi stories

People who completed the survey

People who commented that they were interested

Local pet-community pages

Apartment-community pages

Local pet events

Dog-training and veterinary pages

Followers introduced through mutual contacts

I should not assume that every person who follows a pet page wants to receive a sales message.

A suitable first message should ask for permission.

Example Instagram message

Hello [Name], I am researching the pet-care problems faced by pet parents in [City].

I noticed your interest in local pet-related content. Would you be comfortable answering a few questions about dog walking, sitting or boarding? This is a short research discussion, not a forced sales call.

If the person does not reply, I should not repeatedly message them.

Source 3: Dog Parks and Pet-Friendly Public Areas

I may visit a dog park, pet café, veterinary waiting area, grooming centre or pet-friendly community event.

Before approaching someone, I should:

Wait until the person is not managing a difficult situation

Introduce myself clearly

Explain that the discussion is for research

Ask whether they have five minutes

Avoid disturbing the pet

Avoid taking photographs without permission

Request contact details only when the person agrees to a follow-up

Example introduction

Hello, I am working on a local pet-care project called PetSaathi. We are studying how pet parents arrange dog walking, sitting and boarding. Would you be comfortable answering a few short questions?

Source 4: Personal Contacts and Referrals

I will contact:

Friends who own pets

Relatives who own pets

Colleagues with pets

Neighbours

Former classmates

Veterinary contacts

Groomers

Dog trainers

Existing pet-community contacts

I should also ask every suitable respondent:

Do you know one or two other pet parents who may be willing to participate in this research?

This creates referral-based outreach and can produce more relevant leads than random cold messaging.

Lead-list information

Before beginning outreach, I will prepare a simple list containing:

### Table 34

| Field | Purpose |
| --- | --- |
| Lead ID | Unique customer reference |
| Name | Identifies the respondent |
| Area | Confirms geographical suitability |
| Lead source | Shows how the person was found |
| Pet type | Helps identify service relevance |
| Contact permission | Records whether outreach is permitted |
| Preferred channel | WhatsApp, call or Instagram |
| First contact status | Tracks whether contact was attempted |
| Notes | Records useful context |

Only necessary information should be collected. India’s DPDP Act states that consent should be specific, informed and limited to personal data necessary for the stated purpose. It also provides for withdrawal of consent, so a person who asks PetSaathi to stop contacting them should be marked accordingly.

Output

A list of at least 20 relevant and responsibly sourced pet-parent leads.

Simple explanation for professor

“I identified twenty potential pet parents through societies, social media, public pet communities and personal referrals while avoiding unapproved bulk messaging.”

10:00–12:00 — Message or Call the First Ten Pet Parents

During this session, I will contact the first group of ten pet parents.

I should use the communication method preferred by each person.

A complete conversation may take approximately 10–15 minutes, while the initial message should remain short.

Initial WhatsApp message

Hello [Name],

I am Prince, and I am working on PetSaathi, a proposed service for verified dog walking, pet sitting and controlled pet boarding.

I am speaking with pet parents in [Area] to understand the problems they face while arranging pet care.

Would you be available for a short 10-minute call or WhatsApp conversation? This is primarily a research discussion.

Opening the conversation

At the beginning of the call, I will say:

Thank you for speaking with me. I am studying how pet parents currently manage walking, sitting and boarding. I would like to understand your real experience. I may also explain a small paid pilot at the end, but there is no obligation to join.

This introduction makes the purpose of the discussion clear.

Questions about the pet

I will ask:

What type of pet do you have?

What is the pet’s age and breed?

Which area or society do you live in?

Who normally cares for the pet when you are busy?

How often do you need outside pet-care assistance?

These questions help determine whether the respondent matches the target customer group.

Questions about previous behaviour

I will ask questions about real past experiences rather than hypothetical opinions.

Examples include:

When was the last time you needed a walker, sitter or boarding service?

How did you find the caregiver?

How much did you pay?

What was the most difficult part?

Was anything disappointing or unsafe?

What happened when the regular caregiver was unavailable?

Have you ever cancelled travel because pet care was not available?

Past behaviour usually provides stronger validation than asking only whether a person “likes” an idea.

Questions about trust

I will ask:

What makes you trust a pet-care provider?

What would make you reject a sitter immediately?

Is identity verification important?

Would you prefer to meet the sitter first?

Would photographs or location updates be useful?

Would you value a pet report card?

How important is emergency-veterinary support?

Would you prefer the same sitter for repeat bookings?

I should not argue with the respondent when they express concerns.

The purpose is to understand the concern accurately.

Questions about pricing

I will ask:

How much do you currently pay for pet care?

What price would feel reasonable for a 30-minute walk?

What price would feel reasonable for one hour of pet sitting?

What price would you expect for overnight boarding?

Would you pay more for a caregiver who completed additional verification?

Would you prefer per-service pricing or a monthly package?

I should first ask about the respondent’s existing spending before showing the PetSaathi price.

Payment-validation question

At the end of the discussion, I will present a clear offer to suitable respondents.

Example

We are considering a limited 30-minute dog-walking trial in [Area] for ₹99–₹149. The trial would include an approved walker, start and end confirmation and a short walk report. Would you consider booking this trial during the next seven days?

Possible responses should be recorded as:

Ready to book

Interested, but needs more information

Interested at a later date

Price is too high

Price appears suspiciously low

Needs to meet the sitter first

Not interested

Service is not currently required

Conversation-closing statement

Thank you for sharing your experience. May I contact you again if the pilot becomes available in your area?

The answer should be recorded in the CRM.

Output

Ten initial outreach attempts and structured notes from the customers who respond.

Simple explanation for professor

“I contacted the first ten pet parents and asked about their real experiences, trust concerns, budgets and willingness to book a paid trial.”

12:00–13:00 — Update the CRM

During this session, I will transfer all information from the morning conversations into the CRM.

The CRM should be updated immediately so important information is not forgotten.

Required CRM fields

### Table 35

| CRM field | Information to record |
| --- | --- |
| Lead ID | Example: PP-001 |
| Date contacted | Date of outreach |
| Name | Customer’s name |
| City | Customer’s city |
| Area | Customer’s locality |
| Society | Residential society |
| Pet type | Dog, cat or other |
| Pet details | Breed, age and relevant context |
| Service required | Walking, sitting or boarding |
| Service frequency | Daily, weekly, travel or emergency |
| Current solution | Family, helper, sitter or boarding facility |
| Last paid price | Previous service spending |
| Expected budget | Acceptable price |
| Main problem | Primary customer pain |
| Main trust concern | Barrier to purchase |
| Trial response | Yes, maybe or no |
| Contact permission | Follow-up allowed or not |
| Lead status | New, hot, warm or cold |
| Last contact | Most recent communication |
| Next action | Follow-up task |
| Notes | Important observations |

Data-quality rules

I will ensure that:

Every lead receives one unique ID.

Telephone numbers are not copied into public sheets.

Access is limited to relevant team members.

Personal opinions are not presented as facts.

Sensitive medical information is not collected unless necessary.

Customers who decline follow-up are clearly marked.

Duplicate records are merged or linked.

The customer’s own wording is preserved in the notes when useful.

Example CRM record

### Table 36

| Field | Example |
| --- | --- |
| Lead ID | PP-004 |
| Area | Whitefield |
| Pet | Three-year-old Labrador |
| Requirement | Morning dog walking |
| Current solution | Domestic helper |
| Main problem | Helper is frequently unavailable |
| Trust concern | Unknown walker handling a strong dog |
| Budget | ₹150–₹250 |
| Trial response | Interested after meeting sitter |
| Status | Warm |
| Next action | Share sitter process and follow up Friday |

Output

An updated CRM containing complete and usable notes from the morning outreach.

Simple explanation for professor

“I recorded every conversation in the CRM so the team can track customer needs, objections, follow-up permissions and booking readiness.”

13:00–14:00 — Lunch Break

I will take a lunch break after completing the first ten outreach attempts and updating the CRM.

Before the break, I will review the morning results and identify any weakness in the interview approach.

I will check:

Which question customers misunderstood

Which message received the best response

Whether the introduction was too long

Whether customers understood the service

Whether the price question was clear

Whether important information was missing

Which objections appeared repeatedly

Output

No new outreach output is required during this period, but the afternoon approach should be improved using the morning findings.

14:00–16:00 — Message or Call the Next Ten Pet Parents

During this session, I will contact the second group of ten pet parents.

I will use the same core interview structure so the answers remain comparable.

However, I may improve the wording if the first ten respondents found a question confusing.

Afternoon outreach process

For each person, I will:

Confirm that I have an appropriate reason or permission to contact them.

Introduce PetSaathi clearly.

Ask whether they have time for a short conversation.

Ask about their latest real pet-care experience.

Record their existing solution.

Identify their most serious problem.

Ask about trust and safety expectations.

Ask about previous and acceptable prices.

Present the relevant trial offer.

Request follow-up permission.

Record the result immediately.

Do not change the main offer for every respondent

It is acceptable to adjust minor wording, but the main trial offer should remain consistent.

For example, if one person is offered a 30-minute walk at ₹99 and another identical customer is offered the same service at ₹249 without a defined experiment, the results will be difficult to compare.

If different prices are tested, the experiment should be recorded clearly.

Example price test

Group A: ₹99 trial walk

Group B: ₹149 trial walk

Same location

Same service duration

Same service inclusions

Same qualification rules

This helps the team compare price responses more fairly.

Response-status options

Each afternoon outreach attempt should be recorded as:

Conversation completed

Call scheduled

Asked to receive details by message

No response

Declined conversation

Wrong target customer

Follow-up not permitted

Potential paid-trial customer

Output

Ten additional outreach attempts, producing a total target of twenty pet-parent contacts.

Simple explanation for professor

“I contacted the next ten pet parents using the improved interview script while keeping the service and price tests consistent.”

16:00–17:00 — Mark Hot, Warm and Cold Leads

During this session, I will classify all twenty leads according to customer fit and customer action.

A useful lead score should consider both fit and engagement. HubSpot similarly distinguishes fit criteria, such as customer characteristics, from engagement criteria, such as clicking, responding or taking another action.

Hot lead

A customer should be marked as a hot lead when most of the following conditions are true:

Lives in a selected pilot area

Owns a pet suitable for the pilot

Currently needs walking, sitting or boarding

Requires the service within the next 30 days

Has experienced a real pet-care problem

Accepts the expected price range

Wants to meet or evaluate a sitter

Is ready to select a date

Is willing to pay for a trial

Has given permission for follow-up

Recommended action

Contact the lead within 24 hours and propose a specific next step.

Warm lead

A customer should be marked as a warm lead when:

The person matches the target customer profile

The service may be needed later

The customer is interested but not ready to pay

The customer requires more trust information

The customer is waiting for travel dates

The price is acceptable but the timing is not suitable

The customer wants to meet the sitter before deciding

Recommended action

Record the reason for delay and follow up only at an appropriate time.

Cold lead

A customer should be marked as a cold lead when:

The person does not currently require pet care

The person lives outside the pilot area

The price is far outside the customer’s budget

A permanent trusted solution already exists

The person is not interested

The person does not want further contact

The customer’s requirement does not match the available service

Recommended action

Do not repeatedly contact the lead.

Keep only the useful research insight and respect the customer’s communication preference.

Suggested Phase 1 lead score

### Table 37

| Condition | Points |
| --- | --- |
| Lives in the pilot area | +20 |
| Needs service within 30 days | +20 |
| Has previously paid for pet care | +10 |
| Has an unresolved pet-care problem | +10 |
| Accepts the trial-price range | +15 |
| Selects or suggests a booking date | +10 |
| Agrees to a paid trial | +10 |
| Gives follow-up permission | +5 |

Classification

70–100 points: Hot lead

40–69 points: Warm lead

0–39 points: Cold lead

The score should support judgement rather than replacing it.

Output

Twenty leads classified as hot, warm or cold, with a clear next action for each one.

Simple explanation for professor

“I classified customers according to their location, service need, price acceptance, urgency and willingness to pay.”

17:00–18:00 — Write the Top Customer Objections

During the final session, I will review all twenty conversations and identify the main reasons customers may not book PetSaathi.

An objection is not automatically a rejection.

It is information showing what the customer needs before taking the next step.

Objection 1: “I do not trust an unknown sitter.”

This objection shows that verification and introductions are important.

Possible response:

We understand the concern. The pilot includes an application review and sitter interview. Before confirming the service, we can share the completed verification level and discuss whether a meet-and-greet is possible.

I should not claim that a sitter is completely safe or police-verified unless the relevant process has actually been completed.

Objection 2: “My pet does not accept strangers.”

This concern may require:

A meet-and-greet

A slower introduction

Detailed behaviour information

An experienced sitter

A trial visit before a full service

Some pets may not be suitable for the first pilot.

Objection 3: “The price is too high.”

I should ask:

What price did the customer expect?

What do they currently pay?

Which service features are unnecessary?

Would a package price be more suitable?

Would they accept the price after a successful trial?

The objective is to understand the price gap rather than immediately offer a discount.

Objection 4: “The price is too low to be trustworthy.”

A very low trial price may cause customers to doubt service quality.

The response should explain:

That the price is a limited pilot offer

What is included

Which checks have been completed

What the future regular price may be

Objection 5: “I already have a domestic helper or walker.”

I should ask:

What happens when that person is unavailable?

Does the customer receive updates?

Is there a backup?

Would the customer need support during travel or emergencies?

This objection may reveal an occasional rather than regular requirement.

Objection 6: “I need to meet the sitter first.”

This is a reasonable trust requirement.

PetSaathi should record demand for:

Video introductions

In-person meet-and-greets

Trial visits

Same-sitter requests

Objection 7: “What happens during an emergency?”

The customer may want to know:

Who contacts the veterinarian

Whether transport is available

Who approves expenditure

How the owner is informed

What information the sitter receives

If the emergency process is incomplete, I should state that honestly instead of making an unsupported promise.

Objection 8: “What happens if the sitter cancels?”

This objection shows that backup supply is important.

The customer may expect:

Immediate communication

Replacement-sitter support

A clear refund

Compensation for serious last-minute failure

Objection 9: “I do not want to share my home address.”

The address should not be requested during an early research conversation unless it is necessary.

The area or society may be sufficient for initial qualification.

The complete service address can be collected after the customer decides to proceed.

Objection 10: “I am interested, but not right now.”

I should record the reason and expected future date.

Possible triggers include:

Upcoming travel

Office schedule changes

Festivals

Family events

Regular caregiver leave

Medical recovery

This lead may be warm rather than cold.

Objection-tracking table

### Table 38

| Field | Example |
| --- | --- |
| Objection ID | OBJ-01 |
| Objection | Does not trust strangers |
| Number of mentions | 7 |
| Related service | Dog walking |
| Lead category | Mostly warm |
| Current response | Sitter profile and meet-and-greet |
| Required improvement | Clear verification explanation |
| Owner | Operations team |
| Review date | End of Phase 1 |

Objection-analysis questions

At the end of the day, I will answer:

Which objection appeared most frequently?

Which objection completely stopped bookings?

Which objection could be solved through better communication?

Which objection requires a product feature?

Which objection requires an operational change?

Which objection requires a policy or legal document?

Which objection is specific to one service?

Did customers reject the price or the trust level?

Which customers remain willing to pay?

What should be changed before the next outreach batch?

Output

A ranked list of customer objections and recommended responses or improvements.

Simple explanation for professor

“I reviewed the conversations and recorded the main reasons customers were hesitant, including trust, price, pet behaviour, emergencies and sitter cancellations.”

End-of-Day 4 success checklist

Day 4 is complete when:

Twenty relevant pet parents have been identified.

Twenty outreach attempts have been made.

The number of completed conversations has been recorded.

Every lead has a CRM record.

Follow-up permission has been recorded.

Hot, warm and cold classifications are complete.

Paid-trial interest has been measured.

Customer objections have been ranked.

Follow-up actions are assigned.

People who declined contact are not repeatedly approached.

Recommended Day 4 result summary

At the end of the day, I should prepare a short report containing:

| Paid-trial interest | [Enter result] |

| Top objection | [Enter objection] |
| Average acceptable price | [Enter amount] |
| Follow-ups scheduled | [Enter result] |

Simple explanation for professor

“On Day 4, I conducted the first PetSaathi customer-outreach batch. I first identified twenty pet parents through apartment societies, Instagram, dog parks, personal contacts and referrals. I contacted the first ten customers in the morning and recorded their pet-care problems, existing solutions, trust concerns and price expectations. After updating the CRM, I contacted the next ten customers in the afternoon. I then classified all leads as hot, warm or cold according to their location, urgency, budget and willingness to pay. Finally, I prepared a list of the most common objections so the PetSaathi offer, policies and communication can be improved before the next outreach batch.”

PetSaathi Phase 1 — Day 5: Sitter Outreach Batch 1

Main goal of Day 5

The main goal of Day 5 is to attract at least 15 relevant sitter applicants, understand their experience and availability, shortlist suitable profiles, and schedule formal video interviews.

An applicant should be counted only after completing the sitter application form. A person who only likes, shares, or replies to the recruitment post should be recorded as an enquiry, not as a completed applicant.

Instagram professional accounts provide business tools that can support recruitment posts and audience insights. WhatsApp recruitment messages should be sent only to people who have agreed to receive them, or posted in groups after receiving permission from the group administrator. Meta’s guidance states that businesses should obtain opt-in before initiating marketing messages on WhatsApp.

Day 5 target

By the end of the day, PetSaathi should have:

At least 15 completed sitter applications

A record of every applicant’s source and location

Initial telephone-screening notes

A shortlist of suitable profiles

Rejection or hold reasons for unsuitable profiles

Scheduled video-interview slots

A clear list of remaining recruitment gaps

09:00–10:00 — Post the Sitter Opportunity on Instagram and WhatsApp

During this session, I will publish a clear sitter-recruitment announcement through PetSaathi’s Instagram account, WhatsApp Business account, personal network, society contacts and approved local groups.

The post should explain the opportunity honestly. It should invite people to apply for screening rather than suggesting that every applicant will automatically receive work.

Information that the recruitment post should contain

The post should clearly state:

The PetSaathi brand name

The services for which sitters are required

The launch city and selected areas

The expected availability

The basic applicant requirements

The screening process

The expected earning structure or approximate range

The application link

The application deadline

A contact method for questions

Suitable recruitment heading

Love pets? Apply to become a PetSaathi caregiver. 🐾

Sample Instagram and WhatsApp recruitment post

PetSaathi is inviting responsible pet lovers to apply as dog walkers and pet sitters in selected areas of [City].

We are currently looking for applicants who can provide:

Dog walking

Home pet-sitting visits

Extended pet sitting

Controlled pet boarding, subject to additional checks

Applicants should:

Be comfortable handling pets responsibly

Communicate clearly with pet parents

Be available during their selected time slots

Be willing to provide identity and address verification

Attend a video interview

Complete basic safety training and a service assessment

This is an application opportunity. Final approval depends on screening and service requirements.

Application form: [Insert link]Application deadline: [Insert date]

Important wording rule

The post should say:

“Apply to become a verified PetSaathi sitter.”

It should not say:

“Join as a verified sitter.”

The second statement incorrectly suggests that the person is already verified before the screening process has taken place.

PetBacker’s provider onboarding separates service listing from identity-document verification. Rover also treats a background check as only one part of the wider profile-review process. PetSaathi should follow the same basic principle by separating application, verification, assessment and final approval.

Recommended visual content

The recruitment post may include:

PetSaathi logo

Photograph of a responsible caregiver with a dog

Services available

Pilot areas

Application closing date

“Apply Now” call to action

Short screening-process diagram

Recruitment tracking

Each post should use a trackable source label.

Examples include:

Instagram organic post

Instagram story

WhatsApp personal contact

WhatsApp society group

College group

Veterinarian referral

Groomer referral

Existing sitter referral

This will help PetSaathi identify which recruitment channel produces the best applicants.

Output

A published sitter opportunity across approved Instagram, WhatsApp and community channels.

Simple explanation for professor

“I published a clear sitter-recruitment announcement explaining the services, areas, requirements and screening process.”

10:00–11:30 — Contact Students, Pet Lovers and Local Groups

During this session, I will contact suitable communities and individuals who may be interested in part-time or flexible pet-care work.

The purpose is not to contact random people. The purpose is to identify applicants who live near the launch areas and have a genuine interest in animal care.

Source 1: College and university students

Potential student applicants may be found through:

Veterinary colleges

Animal-science departments

College animal-welfare clubs

Student volunteer groups

Local university communities

Placement or opportunity groups

College societies connected with animal care

Before publishing in a college group, I should obtain permission from the administrator or relevant representative.

The message should explain:

The work is flexible or booking-based

Selection is not automatic

Applicants must complete verification and screening

Availability and service area will affect booking opportunities

No guaranteed income should be promised

Source 2: Existing pet owners and pet lovers

Suitable applicants may include:

People who already own pets

People who regularly care for family pets

Animal-rescue volunteers

Foster caregivers

Pet-community members

Retired pet lovers

Homemakers interested in sitting or boarding

Experienced dog walkers

Trainers or grooming assistants

Owning a pet can provide useful experience, but it should not automatically qualify someone to care for other people’s pets.

Source 3: Local pet groups

Relevant groups may include:

City pet-parent communities

Dog-walking groups

Apartment pet groups

Animal-rescue communities

Pet adoption networks

Breed-specific communities

Local Facebook or WhatsApp groups

Neighbourhood community groups

I should request approval before posting inside a private group.

I should not copy members’ telephone numbers or send repeated unsolicited messages.

Source 4: Veterinary clinics, groomers and trainers

Veterinary clinics, groomers, trainers and pet shops may know responsible people looking for pet-care opportunities.

A suitable request may say:

Hello, I am recruiting dog walkers and pet sitters for the PetSaathi pilot in [Area]. Applicants will complete an application, interview and screening process. Could you share the opportunity with suitable people in your network?

Source 5: Apartment societies

Apartment societies may contain:

Students seeking part-time work

Homemakers interested in home visits

Retired pet owners

Existing walkers

Residents experienced with animals

The society manager should be asked to circulate the opportunity voluntarily rather than sharing residents’ private contact information.

Direct outreach message

Hello [Name],

I am working on PetSaathi, a local pet-care pilot for dog walking, pet sitting and controlled boarding.

We are inviting responsible animal lovers in [Area] to apply as caregivers.

Applicants will complete a form, video interview, verification and basic safety assessment before approval.

Would you be interested in receiving the application details?

If the person says yes, I can send the form.

If the person declines, I should not continue sending recruitment messages.

Information to record during sourcing

For every interested person, I will record:

Name

Contact channel

City

Area

Recruitment source

Service interest

Basic availability

Permission to send the application

Form sent status

Follow-up date

Output

A targeted list of potential sitters from colleges, local communities, pet networks, societies and referrals.

Simple explanation for professor

“I contacted suitable students, pet lovers and local groups to find people who may be interested in becoming PetSaathi caregivers.”

11:30–12:30 — Send the Sitter Application Form

During this session, I will send the structured sitter application form to people who have shown genuine interest.

Google Forms can collect responses, display individual and summary results, and send responses to a linked spreadsheet. For privacy, PetSaathi should not enable a public results summary because applicant responses may contain personal and sensitive information.

Application message

Hello [Name],

Thank you for your interest in becoming a PetSaathi caregiver.

Please complete the sitter application form below:

[Application link]

The form asks about your location, experience, availability and preferred services.

Completing the form does not guarantee approval. Shortlisted applicants will be invited for a video interview and further screening.

Please submit the form by [Date and time].

Section 1: Basic applicant information

The form should collect:

Full legal name

Preferred name

Age

Phone number

WhatsApp number

Email address

City

Area or locality

Occupation

Preferred language

Emergency contact

Section 2: Pet-care experience

The form should ask:

How many years of pet-care experience do you have?

Which animals have you handled?

What pet sizes are you comfortable handling?

Have you cared for another person’s pet?

Have you worked as a walker, sitter, trainer or boarding host?

Have you handled anxious or senior pets?

Have you faced a pet-related emergency?

Can you provide references?

Applicants should be encouraged to describe real experiences rather than simply writing that they “love animals.”

Section 3: Services requested

Applicants may select:

Thirty-minute dog walking

Sixty-minute dog walking

Home pet-sitting visits

Extended pet sitting

Daycare

Overnight boarding

Cat sitting

Senior-pet care

Selecting a service should not mean automatic approval for that service.

Section 4: Availability

The form should collect:

Available days

Morning availability

Afternoon availability

Evening availability

Night availability

Weekend availability

Festival availability

Maximum travel distance

Preferred service areas

Number of bookings the applicant can safely handle

Section 5: Earning expectations

The form should ask:

Expected payout for a 30-minute walk

Expected payout for a 60-minute walk

Expected payout for a one-hour sitting visit

Expected payout for overnight boarding

Preferred payment frequency

Willingness to accept trial-period bookings

The applicant’s expected payout should be compared with PetSaathi’s unit-economics assumptions.

Section 6: Verification readiness

The form should ask whether the applicant can:

Provide government-issued identity proof

Provide address proof

Provide references

Attend a video interview

Complete safety training

Complete a practical handling assessment

Provide police or background-verification documentation when required

Use a smartphone with internet access

Share service updates through the approved process

Identity verification should not be described as the same thing as a complete criminal or police-background check. PetBacker’s documentation and Rover’s process both demonstrate that identity verification, background checking and profile approval are separate stages.

Section 7: Boarding-specific questions

Applicants interested in boarding should answer additional questions about:

Type of home

Owned or rented property

Landlord or society permission

Family members in the home

Children in the home

Existing pets

Secure doors and windows

Balcony or terrace safety

Available sleeping space

Maximum boarding capacity

Emergency transport

Willingness to complete a home assessment

Home photographs may be requested later through a controlled process.

Section 8: Motivation and judgement

The form should ask:

Why do you want to become a pet sitter?

What do you enjoy about animal care?

Which pets are you not comfortable handling?

What would you do if a dog refused to walk?

What would you do if a pet became ill?

What would you do if you could not attend a confirmed booking?

Section 9: Declarations

Applicants should confirm that:

Their information is accurate.

Submitted documents must be genuine.

Approval is not guaranteed.

PetSaathi may verify submitted information.

Only approved services may be offered.

Bookings cannot be transferred to another person.

Customer information must remain confidential.

Serious incidents must be reported immediately.

Application-form privacy rule

For the first open recruitment form, it is safer to ask whether the applicant can provide identity documents rather than asking every unqualified applicant to upload all documents immediately.

Full verification documents can be collected from shortlisted applicants through a controlled, access-restricted process. PetBacker’s document guidance also stresses that submitted identification is used for verification and advises applicants to obscure unnecessary identification numbers.

Output

The application form is sent to interested candidates, and the responses begin entering the sitter CRM.

Simple explanation for professor

“I sent a structured application form to interested people so I could compare their experience, services, availability and verification readiness.”

12:30–13:30 — Lunch Break

I will take a lunch break after completing the initial recruitment and application distribution.

Before the break, I will check:

How many people received the application

How many opened it

How many completed it

Which recruitment sources generated responses

Whether any form question caused confusion

Whether any applicant requested assistance

Whether the target of 15 applications is realistic

Output

No new recruitment activity is required during this time.

13:30–15:00 — Call Interested Sitters

During this session, I will conduct short initial screening calls with applicants who completed the form or showed strong interest.

Each call should last approximately 10–15 minutes.

The call is an initial screening, not the final video interview.

Opening statement

Hello [Name], thank you for applying to become a PetSaathi caregiver.

I would like to ask a few questions about your experience, availability and service preferences.

This is an initial screening call. Final approval will require further verification, an interview and the relevant service assessment.

Question 1: Motivation

Why do you want to become a pet sitter or dog walker?

A strong answer should demonstrate responsibility and realistic expectations, not only a desire for easy money or casual access to animals.

Question 2: Previous experience

Tell me about the pets you have cared for previously.

I should ask for specific examples, including:

Pet type

Pet size

Duration of care

Tasks performed

Difficulties faced

Outcome

Question 3: Service preference

Which PetSaathi services would you like to provide?

I should confirm whether the applicant understands the difference between:

Walking

Home visits

Extended sitting

Daycare

Boarding

Question 4: Availability

Which days and time slots can you reliably commit to?

The applicant’s availability should match real customer demand.

For example, dog-walking demand may be stronger in the morning and evening.

Question 5: Service area

How far are you willing to travel for a booking?

I should record:

Main locality

Maximum travel distance

Transport method

Expected travel cost

Question 6: Safety judgement

I will ask at least one scenario question.

Example:

What would you do if a dog escaped from its collar during a walk?

A suitable answer should include immediate reporting, safe searching, contacting PetSaathi and the owner, and avoiding concealment of the incident.

Question 7: Pet behaviour

How would you approach a nervous dog that does not want you to touch it?

I should listen for patience, distance, owner guidance and avoidance of force.

Question 8: Emergency response

What would you do if a pet suddenly had difficulty breathing or became unconscious?

The applicant should understand that the owner, PetSaathi support and a veterinary professional must be contacted immediately.

Question 9: Reliability

What would you do if you could not attend a confirmed booking?

A suitable applicant should report the problem early and should not secretly send a friend or family member.

Question 10: Customer communication

Are you comfortable sending arrival confirmation, photographs and a final service report?

Pet-care marketplaces commonly use photographs and service updates as part of the sitter-customer experience. PetBacker, for example, promotes photo updates and real-time dog-walk reports for its providers.

Question 11: Verification

Are you comfortable completing identity, address, reference and applicable background-verification steps?

The applicant should be allowed to ask how the documents will be stored and used.

Question 12: Earning expectations

What payout do you expect for the services you selected?

The applicant’s answer should be recorded without immediately promising the requested amount.

Initial call outcomes

After the call, I will mark the applicant as:

Strong potential

Suitable for further interview

Needs more information

Suitable for limited services only

Outside the service area

Availability mismatch

Payout mismatch

Safety concern

Not suitable

Did not attend call

Output

Initial screening notes for interested sitter applicants.

Simple explanation for professor

“I called interested applicants to understand their experience, availability, safety judgement, service preferences and earning expectations.”

15:00–16:30 — Shortlist Suitable Profiles

During this session, I will compare the application forms and screening-call notes.

Applicants should not be shortlisted only because they appear friendly or enthusiastic.

The decision should be based on consistent criteria.

Proposed sitter-shortlisting score

### Table 40

| Criterion | Maximum points |
| --- | --- |
| Location and service-area match | 15 |
| Availability match | 15 |
| Relevant pet-care experience | 20 |
| Safety judgement | 20 |
| Communication ability | 10 |
| Verification readiness | 10 |
| Payout compatibility | 5 |
| Reliability and professionalism | 5 |
| Total | 100 |

Location and service-area match — 15 points

The applicant should live inside or near the selected pilot areas.

Long travel distances may cause delays, cancellations and unsuitable economics.

Availability match — 15 points

The applicant should be available during the time periods when customers actually need the selected service.

Pet-care experience — 20 points

Higher scores should be given for relevant and verifiable experience.

Examples include:

Caring for other people’s pets

Regular dog walking

Volunteering with animals

Fostering pets

Working with a trainer, clinic or boarding provider

Handling different pet sizes safely

Safety judgement — 20 points

The applicant should demonstrate sensible responses to:

Escape risk

Aggression

Illness

Injury

Unsafe equipment

Missed booking

Customer not responding

Communication — 10 points

The applicant should communicate clearly, respectfully and honestly.

Verification readiness — 10 points

The applicant should be willing to complete the required verification stages.

Refusing a reasonable verification process is an important warning sign.

Payout compatibility — 5 points

The applicant’s expectations should be compared with the proposed customer price, platform margin and operational costs.

Reliability — 5 points

The applicant should:

Submit information on time

Attend scheduled calls

Respond clearly

Avoid contradictory information

Follow basic instructions

Proposed classification

75–100 points: Strong shortlist

60–74 points: Conditional shortlist

40–59 points: Hold for review

Below 40 points: Do not proceed

The score should support human judgement rather than automatically approving applicants.

Immediate rejection or escalation indicators

An applicant should not proceed when there is a serious concern such as:

False or inconsistent identity information

Refusal to complete verification

Use of violent animal-handling methods

Intention to send another person for bookings

Repeated disrespectful communication

Unwillingness to report incidents

Dishonest experience claims

Unsafe boarding environment

Intention to accept more pets than can be handled safely

Shortlist output

From 15 applicants, a reasonable first outcome might be:

6–10 shortlisted for video interview

2–4 placed on hold

Remaining profiles rejected or kept for another area or service

These are internal planning estimates, not fixed requirements. Quality is more important than approving a particular number.

Output

A documented shortlist containing scores, suitable services and clear decision reasons.

Simple explanation for professor

“I compared every applicant using the same criteria and shortlisted the people who matched the location, availability, experience and safety requirements.”

16:30–18:00 — Schedule Video Interviews

During this session, I will arrange structured video interviews with shortlisted applicants.

The video interview should provide enough time to evaluate communication, judgement and service suitability in greater depth.

Recommended interview duration

Each interview may be scheduled for approximately 20–30 minutes.

Additional time may be required for boarding applicants or applicants requesting several service categories.

Information to send before the interview

The applicant should receive:

Interview date

Start time

Expected duration

Video platform link

Interviewer’s name

Required preparation

Contact method for technical problems

Rescheduling process

Reminder that the interview does not guarantee approval

Interview scheduling message

Hello [Name],

Thank you for completing the PetSaathi application and initial screening call.

We would like to invite you to a video interview.

Date: [Date]Time: [Time]Duration: Approximately 25 minutesMeeting link: [Link]

Please keep the following ready:

A stable internet connection

Your availability and service-area information

Any questions about the PetSaathi process

The interview is part of the screening process and does not guarantee final approval.

Please reply Confirm or request another available time.

Suggested interview schedule fields

Sitter ID

Interviewer

Meeting link

Confirmation status

Reminder sent

Scheduling rules

I will ensure that:

Applicants receive sufficient notice.

Confirmed times are recorded in the CRM.

A reminder is sent before the interview.

Applicants can request one reasonable reschedule.

Interview preparation

Before each interview, the interviewer should review:

Application form

Requested services

Location

Availability

Experience

Verification readiness

Specific concerns requiring clarification

End-of-day interview targets

By the end of the session, I should aim to have:

All shortlisted candidates contacted

Interview slots offered

Confirmations recorded

Unavailable candidates marked for follow-up

Interviewers and meeting links assigned

Remaining recruitment gaps identified

Output

A complete video-interview schedule for shortlisted sitter applicants.

Simple explanation for professor

“I scheduled formal video interviews with the shortlisted applicants and recorded every interview date, time, confirmation and next action.”

End-of-Day 5 dashboard

At the end of Day 5, I should prepare the following report:

Day 5 success checklist

Day 5 is complete when:

The sitter opportunity has been published.

Group-posting permissions have been respected.

Interested applicants have received the form.

At least 15 completed applications have been targeted.
