After completing the service, the sitter should submit a structured report card.

The report card is important because it converts an informal WhatsApp update into consistent operational data.

Required report-card fields

Booking information

Booking ID

Customer ID

Sitter ID

Service type

Date

Time information

Scheduled start time

Actual start time

Actual end time

Total duration

Delay, if any

Dog-walk information

Approximate distance

Route or tracking status

Toilet activity

Water break

Walking behaviour

Interactions with people or animals

Equipment problems

Pet-sitting information

Food provided

Water changed

Toilet or litter activity

Play or companionship

Pet mood

Home secured

Special instructions completed

Health and behaviour

Energy level

Mood

Unusual behaviour

Visible injury

Vomiting or diarrhoea

Breathing concern

Medication given under instruction

Incident reported

Evidence

Photographs attached

Videos attached

Location evidence attached

Customer update sent

Sitter notes

The sitter should write a short factual summary.

The note should avoid unsupported medical conclusions.

For example:

Appropriate:“Bruno slowed down during the final five minutes but continued walking normally after a short water break.”

Inappropriate:“Bruno was tired because he has a heart problem.”

Only a qualified veterinary professional should make a medical diagnosis.

Improved Manual Pet Report Card

PetSaathi Pet Report Card

Booking ID: BK-001Pet: BrunoService: 30-minute dog walkDate: [Insert date]Sitter: [Sitter name]

Service Timing

Scheduled time: 7:30 AM–8:00 AMActual start: 7:31 AMActual finish: 8:01 AMTotal duration: 30 minutes

Walk Summary

Approximate distance: 1.2 kilometresRoute tracking: CompletedPee: YesPoop: YesWaste collected: YesWater break: Yes

Pet Condition

Mood: Happy and activeEnergy level: NormalWalking behaviour: CalmInteraction with other dogs: No direct interactionHealth concern observed: None

Media

Photographs: AttachedVideos: AttachedLocation update: Shared

Sitter Note

“Bruno walked calmly and responded well to leash guidance. He completed the walk without any safety issue and appeared happy and active throughout the service.”

Incident Status

Incident reported: No

Completion

Report submitted at: 8:08 AMCustomer notified: Yes

Customer-facing short version

Bruno’s walk is complete 🐾

Time: 7:31 AM–8:01 AMDuration: 30 minutesDistance: Approximately 1.2 kmPee: YesPoop: YesWater break: YesMood: Happy and active

Sitter note: Bruno walked calmly and responded well to leash guidance. No safety or health concerns were observed during the walk.

Photos and videos are attached.

Output

A standardised report card sent to the customer after every completed service.

Simple explanation

“I sent the customer a structured report showing the service time, pet activity, mood, updates and sitter observations.”

8. Ask for a Customer Review

After sending the report card, PetSaathi should request an honest review.

The request should be short and should not pressure the customer to provide a positive rating.

Suggested review questions

Ask the customer to rate:

Booking experience

Sitter punctuality

Sitter behaviour

Communication

Quality of updates

Report-card usefulness

Overall satisfaction

Use a rating scale from one to five.

Additional questions

Ask:

What did we do well?

What should we improve?

Would you use PetSaathi again?

Would you request the same sitter?

Would you pay the expected regular price?

May we use your review as a testimonial?

Testimonial permission should be separate from the normal service review.

Reviews from completed services help future customers evaluate pet-care providers. Both PetBacker and Rover use reviews or testimonials as trust signals, but PetSaathi should ensure that pilot reviews come from genuine completed interactions.

Suggested review request

Hello [Name],

Thank you for completing your PetSaathi trial with [Sitter Name].

Please share your honest feedback through this short review form:[Review link]

Your feedback will help us improve future bookings.

Output

A verified post-service review connected to the booking ID.

Simple explanation

“I asked the customer for an honest review after completing the service and sending the report card.”

9. Ask for a Referral

After a successful service, the customer may be asked to introduce another pet parent.

The customer should forward the PetSaathi link themselves rather than sharing someone else’s phone number without permission.

Suggested referral message

Thank you for trying PetSaathi. 🐾

Do you know another pet parent in [Area] who may need dog walking or pet sitting?

You may forward this trial link to them:[Link]

They can contact us directly if interested.

Information to record

The CRM should contain:

Referring customer ID

Referred lead ID

Referral date

Service interest

Payment status

Reward eligibility

Reward provided

Referral timing

Ask for a referral when:

The customer has expressed satisfaction

The service has been completed

No serious complaint is open

The customer has not asked to stop communication

Do not ask for a referral immediately after a poor or disputed service.

Output

Permission-based referral requests sent to satisfied customers.

Simple explanation

“I asked satisfied customers to forward PetSaathi information to other pet parents instead of sharing private contact details.”

Emergency Rule During a Booking

If a pet becomes seriously ill, injured, lost or unsafe, the sitter should stop the normal service process and activate the incident procedure.

The sitter should:

Protect the pet from immediate danger where safely possible.

Contact PetSaathi support.

Contact the pet parent.

Contact the registered emergency person if required.

Contact the veterinarian or emergency hospital.

Follow professional veterinary instructions.

Record the time, location and actions taken.

Submit a complete incident report.

The American Veterinary Medical Association advises contacting the veterinarian or local emergency hospital during an emergency so the facility can prepare for the pet’s arrival.

Suggested Day 11 Plan

Morning

Select one to three low-risk confirmed bookings.

Review pet profiles.

Confirm sitter assignments.

Verify payments.

Send confirmations.

During the Services

Record sitter check-in.

Monitor updates.

Confirm service completion.

Handle any operational issue immediately.

Evening

Collect sitter reports.

Send customer report cards.

Request reviews.

Record operational issues.

Suggested Day 12 Plan

Morning

Review Day 11 problems.

Correct the checklist or message templates.

Confirm the second batch of bookings.

During the Services

Complete additional trial bookings.

Test whether the improved process works.

Compare different sitters or services using the same quality criteria.

Evening

Collect reviews and referrals.

Calculate actual service costs.

Assess repeat-booking interest.

Update sitter-performance records.

Prepare the Days 11–12 report.

Manual Booking Checklist

Before the Service

Pet details confirmed

Behaviour details confirmed

Emergency contact confirmed

Sitter suitability confirmed

Sitter accepted booking

Payment confirmed

Cancellation terms accepted

Customer received sitter details

Instructions shared with sitter

Meet-and-greet completed where required

During the Service

Sitter arrival recorded

Service started

Tracking started where applicable

Customer update sent

Photograph or video provided

Safety instructions followed

Incident escalated if necessary

After the Service

End time recorded

Pet returned or home secured

Report card submitted

Customer notified

Review requested

Referral requested where appropriate

Sitter payout recorded

Booking marked completed

Issues added to the improvement list

Days 11–12 KPI Dashboard

### Table 56

| Metric | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| Paid bookings scheduled | Trials prepared for delivery |
| Bookings completed | Services delivered successfully |
| On-time arrival rate | Sitter reliability |
| Report-card completion rate | Operational discipline |
| Photo/update completion rate | Service-proof consistency |
| Customer rating | Service satisfaction |
| Same-sitter request rate | Customer trust |
| Repeat-booking interest | Future demand |
| Referral rate | Customer advocacy |
| Complaint rate | Service problems |
| Incident count | Safety performance |
| Refund rate | Payment or quality problems |

Simple Explanation for Professor

“During Days 11 and 12, I manually completed PetSaathi’s first paid bookings. Before each service, I confirmed the pet’s health, behaviour and care instructions, matched an appropriate sitter and collected the payment. I shared the sitter’s verified information with the customer and recorded the service start time. During the service, the sitter sent photographs, videos or location updates as agreed. After completion, the sitter submitted a structured pet report card containing the service duration, distance, toilet activity, water break, pet mood and observations. I then requested an honest customer review and asked satisfied customers to refer other pet parents. This manual process helped validate whether PetSaathi could safely manage a complete booking before building automated application features.”

PetSaathi Phase 1 — Day 13: Analyse Validation Results

Main goal of Day 13

The purpose of Day 13 is to convert the information collected during the validation sprint into clear business decisions.

By this stage, PetSaathi may have collected:

Pet-parent conversations

Customer leads

Paid trial bookings

Sitter applications

Completed services

Customer reviews

Partner responses

Website analytics

Payment information

Customer objections

Day 13 is not simply about counting these results. It is about understanding which area, service, price, trust message, sitter type and marketing channel should receive priority.

Google Analytics traffic-source dimensions can show where website users and sessions originated. Manually tagged campaign links can add source, medium and campaign information to reports. However, offline referrals, society introductions and WhatsApp conversations should also be recorded directly in the CRM because some traffic may otherwise appear as direct or unattributed.

Before Starting the Analysis

1. Clean the Data

Before calculating results, I will check the CRM for:

Duplicate customer records

Duplicate sitter applications

Test-form submissions

Internal team visits

Cancelled test payments

Incomplete records

Incorrect lead sources

Missing areas

Missing booking statuses

Inconsistent service names

For example, the following entries should be standardised:

“Dog walk”

“Walking”

“30-minute walking”

“Walk service”

All of these should use one common category such as Dog Walking.

2. Separate Activity from Results

I will separate actions from outcomes.

### Table 57

| Activity | Actual result |
| --- | --- |
| Message sent | Customer replied |
| Payment link sent | Customer paid |
| Sitter form opened | Application submitted |
| Trial booked | Service completed |
| Review requested | Review received |
| Society contacted | Society agreed to a next step |

A high number of messages does not automatically mean the campaign performed well.

The strongest customer-validation sequence is:

Conversation → Interest → Booking readiness → Payment → Completed service → Repeat interest

3. Use Confirmed Payment Information

A payment should count only when its status confirms that the money was successfully received.

Razorpay allows Payment Links to be tracked through their lifecycle and shows their status in the dashboard. Payment Links may also contain a unique reference ID, amount, description and expiry date, which can help connect the payment with the correct PetSaathi booking.

Question 1: Which Area Gave the Most Leads?

Meaning: Identify the Best Launch Zone

This question helps PetSaathi identify the area where customer demand is most concentrated.

However, I should not select an area only because it produced the highest number of enquiries.

The best launch area should ideally have:

A high number of qualified leads

Several booking-ready customers

Paid trial bookings

Suitable sitter availability

Nearby veterinary support

Short travel distances

Society-partnership opportunities

Acceptable operating costs

Step 1: Count Leads by Area

I will group customer records according to area.

Example

### Table 58

| Area | Total leads |
| --- | --- |
| Vesu | 22 |
| Adajan | 17 |
| Pal | 11 |

From this table, Vesu produced the highest number of leads.

However, lead quantity alone is not sufficient.

Step 2: Count Qualified and Paid Leads

I will then compare how many leads became interested, booking-ready and paid customers.

Example

### Table 59

| Area | Leads | Interested | Booking-ready | Paid trials |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Vesu | 22 | 14 | 7 | 4 |
| Adajan | 17 | 9 | 5 | 3 |
| Pal | 11 | 6 | 1 | 0 |

Vesu produced the largest number of leads and paid trials.

Adajan produced fewer total leads but still generated three paid customers.

Pal produced interest but no payment.

Step 3: Calculate Area Conversion Rate

Lead-to-Payment Conversion

Formula:

Paid trials ÷ Total leads × 100

Example

Vesu

4 ÷ 22 × 100 = 18.2%

Adajan

3 ÷ 17 × 100 = 17.6%

Pal

0 ÷ 11 × 100 = 0%

Vesu and Adajan have similar conversion rates, even though Vesu produced more total customers.

Step 4: Calculate Demand Density

PetSaathi should also examine whether several leads come from the same society or nearby societies.

For example:

### Table 60

| Area | Paid trials | Societies involved | Suitable sitters |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Vesu | 4 | 2 | 4 |
| Adajan | 3 | 5 | 2 |
| Pal | 0 | 3 | 1 |

Vesu may be operationally stronger because four paid customers are concentrated inside only two societies and four suitable sitters are available nearby.

Step 5: Examine Operational Difficulty

For every area, I will review:

Average sitter travel time

Average distance between bookings

Number of available sitters

Veterinary support

Customer response time

Society cooperation

Cancellation rate

Travel cost

Time-slot concentration

An area with slightly fewer customers may still be preferable when service delivery is easier and less expensive.

Area Decision

At the end of the analysis, I should classify areas as:

Primary Launch Area

The area with the strongest combination of payment demand, customer density and sitter availability.

Secondary Launch Area

An area showing promising demand but requiring more customers or sitters.

Hold Area

An area with insufficient demand, payment conversion or operational support.

Simple explanation

“I compared the number of leads, qualified customers, paid trials and available sitters in each area. This helped me identify the strongest launch zone rather than selecting an area based only on enquiry volume.”

Question 2: Which Service Had the Most Demand?

Meaning: Select the First MVP Focus

This question helps determine which service should receive the greatest attention in the first PetSaathi MVP.

The three main services are:

Dog walking

Pet sitting

Pet boarding

Demand should be measured using customer actions rather than survey preference alone.

Step 1: Count Service Enquiries

Example

### Table 61

| Service | Enquiries |
| --- | --- |
| Dog walking | 30 |
| Pet sitting | 18 |
| Pet boarding | 14 |

Dog walking produced the highest number of enquiries.

Step 2: Count Booking-Ready Customers

### Table 62

| Service | Enquiries | Booking-ready |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Dog walking | 30 | 12 |
| Pet sitting | 18 | 8 |
| Pet boarding | 14 | 4 |

Dog walking still has the highest absolute number of booking-ready customers.

Step 3: Count Paid and Completed Trials

### Table 63

| Service | Offers sent | Paid trials | Completed trials |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Dog walking | 15 | 7 | 6 |
| Pet sitting | 9 | 4 | 3 |
| Boarding | 5 | 1 | 1 |

Dog walking generated the highest number of paid and completed trials.

Step 4: Calculate Offer-to-Payment Conversion

Formula:

Paid trials ÷ Offers sent × 100

Dog Walking

7 ÷ 15 × 100 = 46.7%

Pet Sitting

4 ÷ 9 × 100 = 44.4%

Boarding

1 ÷ 5 × 100 = 20%

Dog walking has the highest conversion, although pet sitting performs almost as well.

Step 5: Compare Repeat Demand

I will ask customers whether they need the service:

Daily

Several times each week

Weekly

Monthly

Only during travel

Only during emergencies

Dog walking may produce frequent repeat bookings.

Pet sitting may produce fewer bookings but stronger trust relationships.

Boarding may produce higher booking values but lower frequency and greater operational risk.

Step 6: Compare Service Economics

The most requested service is not automatically the best service.

I should compare:

### Table 64

| Metric | Walking | Sitting | Boarding |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Average customer price | — | — | — |
| Average sitter payout | — | — | — |
| Payment cost | — | — | — |
| Support cost | — | — | — |
| Refund cost | — | — | — |
| Net contribution | — | — | — |
| Repeat potential | — | — | — |
| Safety complexity | — | — | — |

For example, boarding may generate more revenue per booking but require more inspections, emergency support and local compliance work.

Service Decision

Primary MVP Service

Select the service with the best combination of:

Paid demand

Repeat need

Operational reliability

Sitter availability

Customer satisfaction

Positive contribution

Secondary Service

Keep a second service available where demand is promising but volume is smaller.

Restricted or Delayed Service

Delay a service when:

Safety requirements are incomplete

Supply is insufficient

Local rules are unclear

Payment conversion is weak

Service complaints are high

Simple explanation

“I compared enquiries, paid bookings, completed services, repeat demand and operating cost. The service with the strongest overall evidence should become the first MVP focus.”

Question 3: Which Price Converted?

Meaning: Identify the Strongest Pricing Signal

This question helps PetSaathi understand which price customers were genuinely willing to pay.

The analysis should compare conversion rates at different price points.

It should not simply compare the number of payments because one price may have been offered to many more people.

Example Dog-Walking Price Test

### Table 65

| Trial price | Offers sent | Payments | Conversion |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| ₹99 | 10 | 6 | 60% |
| ₹149 | 10 | 4 | 40% |
| ₹199 | 10 | 2 | 20% |

In this example, ₹99 produced the highest initial conversion.

However, I should not immediately conclude that ₹99 is the permanent price.

Examine Contribution at Each Price

Assume the sitter payout and operational cost total ₹90.

### Table 66

| Customer price | Approximate cost | Contribution | Paid trials | Total contribution |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| ₹99 | ₹90 | ₹9 | 6 | ₹54 |
| ₹149 | ₹90 | ₹59 | 4 | ₹236 |
| ₹199 | ₹90 | ₹109 | 2 | ₹218 |

Although ₹99 produces more bookings, ₹149 produces the highest total contribution in this example.

Therefore, the best price may not be the lowest price.

Compare Similar Customer Groups

A fair price comparison should use customers with similar:

Locations

Pet requirements

Service duration

Sitter quality

Offer wording

Booking period

Acquisition channels

For example, comparing a ₹99 offer sent to warm referrals with a ₹149 offer sent to cold Instagram leads would not be a clean price test.

The channel and trust level may explain the result instead of the price.

Measure Price Quality

For every price, review:

Offer-to-payment conversion

Cancellation rate

Refund rate

Customer rating

Repeat-booking intention

Sitter payout sustainability

Net contribution

Customer perception

A very low price may create bookings but make customers question quality or make the service financially unsustainable.

Pricing Decision

At the end of Day 13, classify prices as:

Trial Acquisition Price

A temporary price used to reduce first-purchase hesitation.

Normal Pilot Price

A price that customers accept and that supports sitter and operating costs.

Future Regular Price

A higher price to test after trust and service quality have been demonstrated.

Simple explanation

“I compared how many customers received each price, how many paid and how much contribution remained after costs. This helped me identify a price that converts customers without making the service unsustainable.”

Question 4: What Trust Concern Repeated Most?

Meaning: Improve the Landing Page and Customer Process

This question identifies the main reason customers hesitate before booking.

Common trust concerns may include:

Unknown sitter

Incomplete verification

Pet safety

Home security

Sitter cancellation

Emergency handling

No service proof

Poor communication

Boarding environment

Refund uncertainty

Step 1: Categorise Every Objection

Customer statements should be grouped into consistent categories.

Example

### Table 67

| Customer statement | Category |
| --- | --- |
| “How do I know the walker is genuine?” | Verification |
| “My dog does not trust strangers.” | Pet behaviour |
| “What if the sitter does not arrive?” | Reliability |
| “What happens if my dog gets injured?” | Emergency support |
| “I do not want someone inside my house.” | Home security |
| “How do I know the walk happened?” | Service proof |

Step 2: Count the Frequency

Example

### Table 68

| Trust concern | Number of mentions |
| --- | --- |
| Unknown sitter | 14 |
| Sitter cancellation | 8 |
| Emergency support | 7 |
| Service proof | 6 |
| Home privacy | 5 |
| Refund uncertainty | 3 |

In this example, the unknown-sitter concern appeared most frequently.

Step 3: Measure Which Objection Blocked Payment

The most frequently mentioned concern may not be the most damaging concern.

For example:

### Table 69

| Concern | Mentions | Bookings blocked |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Unknown sitter | 14 | 4 |
| Emergency support | 7 | 5 |
| Price | 10 | 3 |

Although fewer customers mentioned emergency support, it stopped more bookings.

Therefore, both frequency and commercial impact should be analysed.

Step 4: Compare Objections by Service

Different services may produce different trust concerns.

Dog Walking

Customers may worry about:

Escape risk

Leash handling

Route proof

Sitter punctuality

Pet Sitting

Customers may worry about:

Home access

Key security

Pet behaviour

Privacy

Boarding

Customers may worry about:

Home environment

Other animals

Overnight supervision

Emergency care

Facility standards

Step 5: Review Website Behaviour

Microsoft Clarity session recordings reconstruct page content and user actions such as clicks, mouse movement and scrolling. These recordings can help determine whether visitors reached the trust section, ignored important information or repeatedly clicked non-interactive elements.

For example, if many visitors leave before reaching the verification explanation, PetSaathi may need to move the trust information closer to the hero section.

Landing-Page Improvements

### Table 70

| Repeated concern | Landing-page improvement |
| --- | --- |
| Unknown sitter | Show exact screening stages |
| No police verification | Explain each badge accurately |
| Pet safety | Add service-safety checklist |
| Sitter cancellation | Explain backup and refund process |
| Emergency concern | Add emergency-response steps |
| No service proof | Show sample report card |
| Home privacy | Explain key and data-handling rules |
| Boarding concern | Show provider assessment process |

Important Trust-Wording Rule

PetSaathi should show only checks that have actually been completed.

For example:

Identity checked

Interview completed

Reference checked

Training completed

Approved for dog walking

The page should avoid unsupported phrases such as:

Completely safe

Fully verified

Zero risk

Guaranteed sitter

Guaranteed emergency care

Simple explanation

“I grouped customer objections, counted how often they appeared and measured which concerns stopped payment. The strongest concern should be answered clearly on the landing page and during the booking process.”

Question 5: Which Sitter Type Performed Best?

Meaning: Improve the Sitter-Supply Strategy

This question helps PetSaathi identify the kind of caregiver who delivers the most reliable service.

Possible sitter types include:

College student

Veterinary student

Homemaker

Retired pet lover

Existing pet owner

Professional walker

Trainer or experienced caregiver

The best sitter should not be selected only by customer rating.

Performance should include safety, reliability, communication, service quality and economics.

Core Sitter Metrics

### Table 71

| Metric | What it measures |
| --- | --- |
| Acceptance rate | Willingness to accept suitable bookings |
| On-time arrival rate | Reliability |
| Cancellation rate | Booking stability |
| Report-card completion | Process discipline |
| Photo/update completion | Customer communication |
| Average rating | Customer satisfaction |
| Complaint rate | Service problems |
| Repeat request rate | Customer trust |
| Incident count | Safety performance |
| Average payout | Supply cost |

Example Sitter-Type Comparison

### Table 72

| Sitter type | Completed bookings | On-time rate | Rating | Repeat requests | Cancellations |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| College student | 4 | 75% | 4.2 | 1 | 1 |
| Veterinary student | 3 | 100% | 4.8 | 2 | 0 |
| Homemaker | 2 | 100% | 4.7 | 1 | 0 |
| Professional walker | 5 | 100% | 4.6 | 4 | 0 |

In this example:

The professional walker completed the most services.

The veterinary student achieved the highest rating.

The homemaker performed reliably but had a very small sample.

The college-student group had one cancellation.

The data is not yet sufficient to declare one group universally superior, but it provides directional evidence.

Suggested Sitter Performance Score

### Table 73

| Criterion | Weight |
| --- | --- |
| Safety and rule compliance | 25% |
| Punctuality | 15% |
| Customer rating | 15% |
| Communication quality | 15% |
| Report-card completion | 10% |
| Cancellation performance | 10% |
| Repeat customer requests | 10% |

Example formula

Safety score × 25% + punctuality score × 15% + rating score × 15% + communication score × 15% + report score × 10% + cancellation score × 10% + repeat score × 10%

Match Sitter Type to Service

One sitter type may not be best for every service.

Dog Walking

A professional walker or physically capable experienced pet owner may perform best.

Pet Sitting

A calm caregiver, veterinary student or experienced pet owner may perform well.

Boarding

A suitable homemaker or experienced boarding host may be appropriate after home and compliance checks.

Special-Care Pets

A veterinary or animal-care student may provide useful knowledge, but they should not be presented as a qualified veterinarian unless legally qualified.

Sitter-Supply Decision

At the end of the analysis, I should decide:

Which sitter profiles should be recruited more actively

Which service each sitter type should provide

Which profiles need additional training

Which applicants should remain restricted

Which payout levels attract strong candidates

Which time slots have insufficient sitter availability

Simple explanation

“I compared sitter types using punctuality, safety, customer ratings, communication, cancellations and repeat requests. This helped identify which caregiver profiles should receive priority in future recruitment.”

Question 6: Which Channel Gave Paid Users?

Meaning: Select the Marketing Focus

This question identifies which outreach source generated customers who actually paid.

Possible channels include:

Instagram

WhatsApp

Apartment societies

Veterinary referrals

Groomer referrals

Personal contacts

Customer referrals

Dog parks

Organic website traffic

Paid advertising

Step 1: Record the Original Lead Source

Every customer record should contain the first known source.

Examples include:

Instagram organic

WhatsApp referral

Vesu Society A

Veterinary Clinic B

Personal network

Google search

Google Analytics uses traffic-source dimensions to show where website visitors and sessions originate. Manual campaign parameters can be added to shared links so specific campaigns can appear in analytics reports.

Step 2: Use Trackable Links

Different links should be created for different channels.

Examples

Instagram

utm_source=instagram&utm_medium=organic&utm_campaign=phase1

Society

utm_source=vesu_society&utm_medium=community&utm_campaign=phase1

Veterinary Partner

utm_source=clinic_a&utm_medium=referral&utm_campaign=phase1

The personal customer information should not be included in these parameters.

Step 3: Compare Channel Funnel Results

Example

### Table 74

| Channel | Leads | Interested | Booking-ready | Paid customers |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Instagram | 25 | 10 | 3 | 1 |
| WhatsApp contacts | 18 | 12 | 6 | 3 |
| Society outreach | 15 | 11 | 7 | 5 |
| Veterinary referral | 5 | 4 | 3 | 2 |
| Dog park | 8 | 4 | 1 | 0 |

In this example, Instagram produced the most leads, but society outreach produced the most paid customers.

Veterinary referrals produced only five leads but converted two into paid customers.

Step 4: Calculate Channel Conversion

Formula:

Paid customers ÷ Channel leads × 100

Instagram

1 ÷ 25 × 100 = 4%

WhatsApp

3 ÷ 18 × 100 = 16.7%

Society Outreach

5 ÷ 15 × 100 = 33.3%

Veterinary Referral

2 ÷ 5 × 100 = 40%

Veterinary referrals have the highest conversion rate, while societies produce the highest paid-customer volume.

Step 5: Calculate Customer-Acquisition Cost

When money was spent on a channel, calculate:

Formula:

Total channel cost ÷ Number of paid customers

Example

If PetSaathi spends ₹1,000 on Instagram promotion and gains two paid customers:

₹1,000 ÷ 2 = ₹500 CAC

If a society campaign costs ₹600 and produces six customers:

₹600 ÷ 6 = ₹100 CAC

The society channel would have the lower customer-acquisition cost in this example.

Step 6: Record First and Final Influences

A customer may discover PetSaathi through Instagram but pay only after a society manager or friend recommends the service.

Therefore, the CRM may contain:

Original lead source

Latest influential source

Payment-conversion source

Referral partner

Campaign

This prevents the entire result from being credited automatically to the final WhatsApp conversation.

Channel Decision

Scale

Increase activity in channels that produce:

Paid users

Low acquisition costs

Suitable local customers

Repeat bookings

Referrals

Improve

Continue testing channels that produce many interested leads but few payments.

The message, trust proof or payment flow may need improvement.

Reduce

Reduce effort on channels that consistently produce:

Irrelevant leads

No responses

No paid customers

High costs

Customers outside the launch area

Simple explanation

“I compared the number of leads and paid customers generated by every source. This helped me identify the channels that produced real revenue rather than only website visits or social-media engagement.”

Recommended Day 13 Analysis Schedule

### Table 75

| Time | Task | Output |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 09:00–10:00 | Clean and verify CRM data | Accurate dataset |
| 10:00–11:00 | Compare areas | Best launch zone |
| 11:00–12:00 | Compare services | MVP service priority |
| 12:00–13:00 | Analyse price conversion | Pricing signal |
| 13:00–14:00 | Lunch | — |
| 14:00–15:00 | Analyse trust objections | Messaging improvements |
| 15:00–16:00 | Review sitter performance | Supply strategy |
| 16:00–17:00 | Compare acquisition channels | Marketing focus |
| 17:00–18:00 | Write decisions and next actions | Day 13 analysis report |

Final Day 13 Decision Table

At the end of the day, I should complete the following table:

### Table 76

| Decision area | Result | Evidence | Decision |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Primary launch area | [Area] | Paid trials, density and sitter supply | Focus operations here |
| Secondary area | [Area] | Interest but weaker conversion | Continue limited testing |
| Primary MVP service | [Service] | Paid and repeat demand | Build first |
| Secondary service | [Service] | Promising but lower volume | Keep manual |
| Winning trial price | ₹[Amount] | Highest sustainable conversion | Test again |
| Main trust concern | [Concern] | Frequency and blocked bookings | Update landing page |
| Best sitter profile | [Type] | Ratings and reliability | Recruit more |
| Best paid channel | [Channel] | Paid users and CAC | Increase outreach |
| Weakest channel | [Channel] | Low conversion | Reduce or redesign |

Important Sample-Size Warning

With only five to ten paid trials, the results should be treated as directional evidence, not final proof.

For example:

One excellent sitter does not prove that all sitters of that type will perform well.

One society producing three customers does not prove that every society will convert.

One successful ₹99 offer does not prove that ₹99 should be the permanent price.

One customer complaint does not automatically mean the service should be removed.

The purpose of Day 13 is to identify the strongest current signals and decide what to test next.

Day 13 Final Report Format

1. Executive Summary

Write a short summary of the most important findings.

Example

“Vesu produced the largest number of paid customers and had the strongest sitter availability. Dog walking generated the highest number of paid and completed trials. The ₹149 offer produced a better contribution than the ₹99 offer while maintaining reasonable conversion. The most common trust concern was uncertainty about the sitter’s background. Professional walkers and veterinary students demonstrated the strongest service performance. Society referrals generated the most paid customers at the lowest acquisition cost.”

2. Main Findings

Record:

Best-performing area

Most requested service

Highest-converting sustainable price

Main trust objection

Best-performing sitter profile

Highest-performing acquisition channel

3. Changes Required

Possible changes include:

Focus the next pilot on one area.

Prioritise dog walking in the MVP.

Keep boarding manually restricted.

Add a sitter-screening explanation near the hero section.

Show a sample pet report card.

Recruit more professional walkers.

Increase society outreach.

Retest ₹149 against ₹199.

Reduce broad Instagram outreach.

4. Next Experiment

Every conclusion should produce a testable next action.

Example

### Table 77

| Finding | Next experiment |
| --- | --- |
| Vesu performed best | Run 20 additional offers in Vesu |
| Dog walking converted best | Test monthly walking package |
| ₹149 appears sustainable | Compare ₹149 and ₹179 |
| Verification concern repeated | Add detailed screening section |
| Society leads converted strongly | Contact five more societies |
| Professional walkers performed best | Recruit ten experienced walkers |

Simple Explanation for Professor

“On Day 13, I analysed the complete PetSaathi validation data. I first cleaned the CRM and removed duplicate or incomplete records. I then compared each area according to leads, booking-ready customers, paid trials and sitter availability to identify the strongest launch zone. I compared walking, sitting and boarding using paid demand, service completion, repeat interest and operating cost to select the first MVP focus. I reviewed price conversion and contribution to identify a sustainable pricing signal. I grouped customer objections to find the most important trust concern and used the result to improve the landing page. I also compared sitter types according to safety, punctuality, reviews and cancellations. Finally, I identified which marketing channels generated paid customers rather than only enquiries. These findings will determine PetSaathi’s launch area, MVP service, pricing, sitter-recruitment strategy and marketing focus.”

PetSaathi Phase 1 — Day 14: Improve the Landing Page and Outreach

Main goal of Day 14

The purpose of Day 14 is to improve the parts of PetSaathi that are preventing customers from understanding, trusting or booking the service.

Day 14 is not a complete redesign day. The team should preserve the existing page structure and modify only the elements supported by customer interviews, booking data, GA4 events and Microsoft Clarity observations.

GA4 allows important actions—such as CTA clicks, form submissions and paid-booking confirmations—to be marked as key events. Clarity heatmaps show where visitors click, while session recordings help reveal scrolling, repeated clicks, confusing navigation and other behavioural problems.

Core improvement rule

The Day 14 process should follow this sequence:

Find the problem → identify supporting evidence → make one focused change → test the change → record the result

Changing several major elements simultaneously makes it difficult to understand which modification caused an improvement or decline.

For PetSaathi’s current low-volume pilot, a controlled before-and-after comparison may be more practical than a statistically formal A/B test. The existing version should still be saved so that performance can be compared and the change can be reversed if necessary.

Web performance guidance similarly recommends comparing an improved version against the previous version and continuing to monitor the result for regressions.

Recommended Day 14 Schedule

### Table 78

| Time | Task | Output |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 09:00–09:45 | Review Day 13 evidence | Prioritised problem list |
| 09:45–10:45 | Improve headline if required | Clearer value proposition |
| 10:45–11:45 | Adjust pricing presentation if required | Revised pricing test |
| 11:45–12:45 | Strengthen trust section if required | Improved safety communication |
| 12:45–13:45 | Lunch | — |
| 13:45–14:45 | Improve CTA if required | Better conversion path |
| 14:45–15:45 | Refine area targeting | Focused micro-market message |
| 15:45–16:45 | Test tracking and forms | No broken analytics or flow |
| 16:45–17:30 | Review outreach messages | Updated outreach copy |
| 17:30–18:00 | Approval and change log | Approved Day 14 version |

09:00–09:45 — Review the Evidence Before Editing

Before changing the landing page, I will review the findings from Day 13.

The review should answer:

Which section created confusion?

Which customer objection appeared repeatedly?

Which CTA received the fewest clicks?

Which price produced payment?

Where did visitors leave the page?

Which area produced paid customers?

Which outreach message generated responses?

Which changes can be made without altering the whole system?

Sources of evidence

Customer interviews

Review repeated customer statements such as:

“I do not understand what PetSaathi does.”

“I thought you only provide boarding.”

“How are the sitters verified?”

“The price looks too cheap.”

“I cannot see whether my area is covered.”

“I did not know which button to click.”

CRM information

Review:

Interested leads

Booking-ready leads

Paid customers

Main objections

Requested services

Customer areas

Lead sources

GA4 information

Review:

CTA-click events

Form-open events

Form-submission events

WhatsApp clicks

Payment-link clicks

Key-event completion

GA4 key events are intended to represent actions that are particularly important to business success, making them suitable for measuring actions such as trial requests or completed lead forms.

Microsoft Clarity information

Review:

Click maps

Scroll maps

Session recordings

Dead clicks

Rage clicks

Quick returns

Excessive scrolling

Clarity defines dead clicks as clicks that produce no visible response, while rage clicks indicate repeated clicking within a small area. These patterns can identify broken links, misleading elements or frustrated visitors.

Output

A prioritised list containing only the problems that have sufficient evidence.

Change 1 — Modify the Headline Only When Users Are Confused

What this instruction means

The headline should be changed only when customers cannot quickly explain what PetSaathi offers.

The headline does not need to be rewritten merely because the team has found a more creative phrase.

A clear headline should communicate:

Who the service is for

What service is available

What makes it valuable

Where it is currently available, when relevant

Evidence that the headline is unclear

The headline may require improvement when:

Several testers describe PetSaathi incorrectly.

Visitors repeatedly ask what the company does.

Customers assume PetSaathi is only a directory.

Customers do not notice dog walking or pet sitting.

Visitors leave before viewing the service section.

The headline uses abstract words without explaining the offer.

The page receives traffic but very few service or booking clicks.

One confused visitor is not enough to justify a complete rewrite. The change should be based on a repeated pattern.

Weak headline example

Your pet’s perfect companion

This headline feels friendly, but it does not explain whether PetSaathi provides walking, sitting, boarding, veterinary care or pet products.

Improved headline example

Trusted dog walking and pet sitting for busy pet parents in [Area]

This version clearly communicates:

The customer group

The main services

The trust position

The operating area

Premium version

Verified pet care when work, travel or emergencies keep you away

Supporting sentence:

Book screened dog walkers and pet sitters in selected areas, with service updates and structured pet report cards.

Headline change rules

When improving the headline:

Keep it short.

Name the main service.

Avoid unsupported promises.

Avoid unclear startup terminology.

Keep one primary customer benefit.

Mention the limited pilot where necessary.

Do not change the logo, brand colours and complete hero layout at the same time.

What to measure afterward

Compare:

Hero CTA click rate

Service-section engagement

Form-open rate

WhatsApp enquiries

Correct understanding during user testing

Number of visitors asking basic clarification questions

Output

A clearer headline while preserving the current hero layout.

Simple explanation

“I changed the headline only when customer evidence showed that visitors did not understand the service.”

Change 2 — Modify Pricing Only When It Is Too High, Too Low or Unclear

What this instruction means

Pricing should not be changed because a small number of customers request a discount.

The team should examine whether pricing is:

Preventing suitable customers from booking

Creating doubts about service quality

Failing to cover sitter and operating costs

Being presented unclearly

Missing important inclusions or conditions

Evidence that the price may be too high

The price may be too high when:

Qualified customers repeatedly reject it.

Payment links are opened but not completed.

Customers accept the service but stop at the price.

Comparable customers consistently name a lower acceptable range.

The trial conversion is much lower than expected.

Customers cannot see enough value in the included features.

A price objection may also be a trust objection disguised as a price objection.

For example, a customer may reject ₹199 because they have not seen the sitter profile, not because ₹199 itself is unaffordable.

Evidence that the price may be too low

The price may be too low when:

Customers question sitter quality.

Customers assume the service is informal.

Sitter payout cannot be covered.

Every booking produces a negative contribution.

The low price attracts unsuitable or non-serious customers.

Customers accept the trial but refuse the realistic future price.

Pricing calculation

The minimum evaluation should include:

Customer price − sitter payout − payment cost − support cost − travel subsidy = contribution

Example

### Table 79

| Item | Amount |
| --- | --- |
| Customer price | ₹99 |
| Sitter payout | ₹75 |
| Payment and communication cost | ₹8 |
| Operational support cost | ₹10 |
| Contribution | ₹6 |

A ₹99 price may be acceptable as a limited acquisition experiment, but it may not be suitable as the permanent price.

Do not change price and service together

Suppose the original offer is:

30-minute walk for ₹149 with a photograph and report card

A clean price experiment would test:

30-minute walk for ₹179 with the same inclusions

It would be difficult to interpret the result if the team simultaneously changes:

Price

Duration

Sitter quality

Number of updates

Refund policy

Target area

Pricing-presentation improvement

Sometimes the price is acceptable, but customers do not understand what it includes.

Weak presentation

Dog walking — ₹149

Improved presentation

30-minute trial walk — ₹149

Includes:

Assigned screened walker

Arrival and completion confirmation

One service update

Toilet and water update

Short walk report

This improves perceived value without immediately reducing the price.

Suggested price decisions

Keep the price

Keep the current price when it converts adequately and produces a sustainable contribution.

Reduce the price temporarily

Reduce it only as a defined pilot experiment with a clear end date.

Increase the price

Increase it when the price appears suspiciously low or fails to cover service delivery.

Add a package

Test a package when regular customers want repeated walks.

Example:

One walk: ₹179

Five-walk starter package: ₹799

Monthly package: test after repeat demand is established

Output

A pricing test based on customer conversion and unit economics rather than guesswork.

Simple explanation

“I changed pricing only when payment data, customer objections or service economics showed that the existing price was unsuitable.”

Change 3 — Improve the Trust Section When Customers Fear Safety

What this instruction means

The trust section should be improved when safety concerns repeatedly stop customers from booking.

The objective is not to add general words such as “safe,” “secure” or “verified.”

The objective is to provide specific evidence explaining how risk is managed.

Common trust concerns

Customers may ask:

Who is the sitter?

Has their identity been checked?

Can I meet the sitter first?

What if my dog escapes?

What if the sitter cancels?

What happens during an emergency?

How do I know the walk happened?

What happens to my house key?

Can I request the same sitter again?

Who sees my home address?

Weak trust section

Your pet is always completely safe with us.

This statement is too broad and cannot be guaranteed.

Improved trust section

Screened for the Assigned Service

Each caregiver completes the applicable application, identity review, interview and service assessment before receiving approved bookings.

Meet Before the First Service

A video introduction or meet-and-greet may be arranged for suitable first bookings.

Clear Service Proof

Customers receive the updates included in the booking, such as arrival confirmation, photographs, walk information and a pet report card.

Emergency Escalation Process

During a serious incident, the sitter contacts PetSaathi, the pet parent, the registered emergency contact and the relevant veterinarian according to the documented protocol.

Backup and Cancellation Support

If the assigned sitter becomes unavailable, PetSaathi will attempt to arrange a suitable replacement or apply the stated refund policy.

Add evidence instead of promises

Useful trust evidence may include:

Exact sitter badge definitions

Sample sitter profile

Sample report card

Screening-process diagram

Emergency-process summary

Refund-policy link

Meet-and-greet option

Real customer review

Customer-support contact

The page should display only checks that have genuinely been completed.

For example:

Identity checked

Interview completed

Reference checked

Training completed

Approved for dog walking

Do not display “police verified” unless that specific verification has been completed.

Where the trust section should appear

When safety is the main booking barrier, the most important trust proof should appear near:

The hero section

The primary CTA

Pricing

The booking form

The sitter-confirmation message

It should not be hidden only near the bottom of the page.

What to measure afterward

Compare:

Trust-section scroll reach

CTA clicks after viewing trust information

Number of verification questions

Booking-ready lead count

Payment conversion

Meet-and-greet requests

Safety-related objections

Clarity scroll and session filters can help determine whether users reach the trust information and whether they leave before seeing it.

Output

A stronger trust section containing specific procedures and proof instead of broad promises.

Simple explanation

“I improved the trust section when customers repeatedly expressed concerns about sitter identity, pet safety, emergencies or service proof.”

Change 4 — Improve the CTA When Visitors Do Not Click It

What this instruction means

A call to action, or CTA, tells visitors what they should do next.

PetSaathi’s primary CTAs are:

Book a Trial

Become a Sitter

Partner Your Society

A CTA should be changed when customers understand the service but fail to begin the relevant action.

Evidence of a CTA problem

The CTA may require improvement when:

Visitors reach the hero section but do not click.

The button receives very few clicks.

Users click non-interactive text instead.

Visitors scroll repeatedly looking for the booking option.

Users ask how to book.

Mobile users cannot see or tap the button.

Multiple buttons compete for attention.

The CTA opens the wrong form.

The button text does not explain the next step.

Clarity click maps show which elements receive clicks, while dead-click and rage-click information can reveal elements that appear interactive but do not respond correctly.

Weak CTA examples

Learn More

Explore

Get Started

Click Here

These labels do not clearly explain what happens next.

Improved CTA examples

For pet parents

Check Trial Availability

Book a ₹99 Trial Walk

Request a Sitter Match

View Available Trial Dates

For sitter applicants

Apply as a PetSaathi Sitter

For societies

Request a Society Pilot

Improve only one CTA element at a time

Possible improvements include:

Change the button label.

Move the button higher.

Repeat the CTA after pricing.

Increase the tap area.

Add a short reassurance line.

Fix a broken destination.

Reduce competing buttons.

Add the trial price to the label.

Do not simultaneously change the label, colour, position, form and complete hero design unless the team has approved a broader redesign.

CTA reassurance example

Check Trial Availability

No automatic booking. We will first confirm your area, pet requirements and sitter availability.

This reduces the fear that clicking the button creates an immediate payment obligation.

Track the complete CTA funnel

Measure:

Landing-page visitors

CTA clicks

Form opens

Form starts

Form submissions

Qualified leads

Payment links sent

Paid trials

A high CTA-click rate with a low form-completion rate indicates that the problem may be the form rather than the button.

Output

A clearer CTA and a measurable conversion path.

Simple explanation

“I changed the CTA only when analytics or user observations showed that visitors understood the offer but did not begin the booking process.”

Change 5 — Refine Area Targeting When Leads Are Scattered

What this instruction means

PetSaathi should focus on areas where customer demand and sitter supply overlap.

The team should not continue advertising equally across an entire city when leads are widely scattered and difficult to serve.

Evidence of scattered demand

Area targeting may require improvement when:

Leads come from many distant areas.

Only one customer exists in each locality.

Sitters must travel long distances.

Travel costs reduce the contribution.

Customers request times that cannot be combined.

Society demand is concentrated in only one or two locations.

Paid customers mainly come from one micro-market.

Compare areas properly

For every area, review:

### Table 80

| Metric | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| Total leads | Initial demand |
| Interested leads | Meaningful interest |
| Booking-ready leads | Near-term opportunity |
| Paid trials | Financial validation |
| Suitable sitters | Supply availability |
| Society interest | Community-level opportunity |
| Travel time | Operational difficulty |
| Contribution | Financial quality |
| Repeat demand | Long-term potential |

Focus-area decision

Primary area

The location with the best combination of:

Paid demand

Customer density

Sitter availability

Short travel distances

Partner support

Secondary area

A promising area that can remain open with limited availability.

Waitlist area

An area where demand exists but current sitter supply or density is insufficient.

Landing-page area update

Weak wording

Available across [City]

This may create enquiries that PetSaathi cannot serve.

Improved wording

Currently accepting pilot requests in [Area 1] and [Area 2].

Residents outside these areas may join the waitlist for future expansion.

Outreach-area update

The team should concentrate:

WhatsApp outreach

Society partnerships

Instagram location content

Sitter recruitment

Veterinary partnerships

Paid advertising

on the highest-performing micro-market.

GA4 traffic-acquisition reports can show the sources and campaigns that produced website sessions, while the CRM should remain the main source for customer locality and paid-booking area.

Output

A narrower and more operationally efficient launch-area strategy.

Simple explanation

“I focused outreach on the areas where paid customer demand, suitable sitters and local partnerships were concentrated.”

Improve Outreach Messages Without Rewriting Everything

Day 14 should also update the outreach message when customer evidence shows that the current message is unclear.

Original general message

We are launching a verified pet-care service in [Area]. Would you like to try it?

This message may be too general.

Improved customer message

Hello [Name],

PetSaathi is running a limited pet-care pilot in [Area].

We currently offer a 30-minute dog-walking trial for ₹149, including an assigned screened walker, arrival and completion confirmation, one update and a short walk report.

Would you like to check the available dates this week?

Reply:1 — Share available dates2 — I need more information3 — Not required currently

This message gives the customer:

A clear service

A clear location

A clear price

Clear inclusions

One simple next action

A respectful decline option

Trust-focused version

Hello [Name],

PetSaathi is testing a dog-walking service in [Area]. Before confirmation, we share the assigned sitter’s completed screening level and the full service conditions.

The trial includes arrival confirmation, one service update and a final walk report.

Would you like to review an available sitter and date?

Use this version when sitter trust is the main repeated objection.

Area-focused version

Hello [Name],

PetSaathi is currently accepting a limited number of dog-walking trial requests from residents of [Society/Area].

Keeping the pilot local helps us provide closer sitter availability and more consistent support.

Would you like to receive the trial details?

Use this version when local density is the main strategy.

Major Redesign Requires Approval

What counts as a major redesign?

The following changes should not be implemented without explicit approval:

Replacing the complete brand identity

Changing the PetSaathi name or tagline direction

Rebuilding the complete page structure

Adding or removing a main service

Replacing all navigation

Moving from a landing page to a marketplace interface

Rebuilding all forms

Changing the complete booking workflow

Introducing a new payment model

Creating a new customer segment

Replacing the technology stack

Changing the complete colour and typography system

Adding major features such as accounts, dashboards or live in-app tracking

These changes affect business scope, development effort and the ability to compare the previous version with the new version.

What does not normally require major redesign approval?

Small evidence-based improvements may include:

Revising one headline

Clarifying one service description

Changing a CTA label

Reordering trust points

Adding a sample report card

Updating trial prices

Changing supported areas

Correcting a broken link

Improving mobile spacing

Adding one FAQ answer

These changes preserve the core page and validation system.

Approval request format

When a major redesign appears necessary, prepare the following proposal:

### Table 81

| Field | Explanation |
| --- | --- |
| Problem | What is failing? |
| Evidence | Which customer or analytics data proves it? |
| Proposed redesign | What major change is requested? |
| Expected benefit | What result should improve? |
| Cost | Time, money and development effort |
| Risk | What could become worse? |
| Alternative | Can a smaller change solve it? |
| Approval | Approved, rejected or revised |

Example

Problem: Customers cannot understand the difference between sitting and boarding.

Evidence: Eight of twenty interviews confused the two services, and both cards generate low CTA activity.

Small change attempted: Service descriptions and FAQs were clarified.

Proposed major change: Separate sitting and boarding into individual landing pages.

Expected benefit: Clearer service understanding and better conversion tracking.

Approval required: Yes.

Day 14 Change Log

Every modification should be documented.

### Table 82

| Change ID | Page element | Old version | New version | Evidence | Date | Result |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| CH-001 | Hero headline | Trusted pet care | Dog walking and pet sitting in Vesu | Users confused about service | Day 14 | Pending |
| CH-002 | Primary CTA | Get Started | Check Trial Availability | Low CTA clicks | Day 14 | Pending |
| CH-003 | Trust section | Fully verified sitters | Exact screening stages | Verification objection | Day 14 | Pending |
| CH-004 | Area copy | Available in Surat | Pilot in Vesu and Adajan | Leads scattered | Day 14 | Pending |

The original version should be saved before deployment.

Post-Change Testing Checklist

Headline

Can a new visitor explain the service correctly?

Is the primary service visible?

Is the pilot area clear?

Are unsupported claims removed?

Pricing

Are duration and inclusions visible?

Is the complete price understandable?

Does the price cover basic costs?

Are pilot conditions stated?

Trust

Are the screening stages specific?

Is the emergency process explained?

Is a sample report available?

Are verification claims accurate?

CTA

Is the button visible on mobile?

Does it open the correct form?

Is the next step clear?

Is the click event recorded?

Area targeting

Are active areas clearly listed?

Is a waitlist offered for unsupported areas?

Does outreach focus on the strongest zones?

Are local sitters available?

Technical quality

GA4 events still work.

Clarity still records the correct page.

Forms open and submit successfully.

WhatsApp links work.

No mobile layout has broken.

Page speed has not declined significantly.

Day 14 Decision Rules

Keep the change

Keep the modification when:

Visitor understanding improves.

CTA activity increases.

Form submissions increase.

Trust objections decrease.

Payment conversion improves.

No important metric declines.

Continue testing

Continue the test when:

The early result is promising but the sample is too small.

Customer feedback is mixed.

One area performs differently from another.

The change improves clicks but not payments.

Reverse the change

Return to the previous version when:

Understanding becomes worse.

CTA clicks decrease significantly.

Customers misunderstand the offer.

Unsupported promises have been introduced.

The booking flow breaks.

Mobile usability declines.

Request major-redesign approval

Request approval when the evidence shows that small modifications cannot solve the underlying structural problem.

Final Day 14 Output

At the end of Day 14, PetSaathi should have:

A saved baseline version

A prioritised improvement list

A clearer headline, where required

Revised pricing presentation, where required

Stronger trust evidence, where required

A clearer CTA, where required

More focused area targeting

Updated outreach messages

Working analytics and forms

A complete change log

A documented list of any major redesign requests

Simple Explanation for Professor

“On Day 14, I improved the PetSaathi landing page and outreach using evidence collected during the validation sprint. I did not redesign the complete website. I changed the headline only when customers did not understand the service. I changed pricing only when customer conversion or unit economics showed that it was too high, too low or unclear. I strengthened the trust section when safety concerns repeatedly blocked bookings. I improved the CTA when analytics showed that visitors were not clicking or beginning the booking process. I also narrowed area targeting when customer demand was too widely scattered. Every change was recorded and tested separately. Any major redesign affecting the brand, page structure, services, technology or booking process was documented and submitted for approval before implementation.”

PetSaathi Phase 1 — Day 15: Go / No-Go Decision

Main goal of Day 15

The purpose of Day 15 is to decide whether PetSaathi has collected enough real evidence to move from the Validation Sprint into Phase 2 MVP development.

The decision should not be based on enthusiasm, website appearance, Instagram followers or the number of messages sent. It should be based on customer payments, qualified demand, sitter quality, operational performance and local market density.

A paid customer is particularly strong validation. Y Combinator has described delivering a product to a paying customer as a “gold standard” signal, while Stripe recommends testing an MVP with real customers before committing substantial time and money to a complete product.

The following numbers are PetSaathi’s internal validation targets. They are not universal industry benchmarks.

Day 15 Decision Dashboard

### Table 83

| Metric | Target | What it proves |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Paid bookings | 5–10 | Real willingness to pay |
| Booking-ready leads | 20+ | Near-term customer pipeline |
| Interested pet parents | 50+ | General market interest |
| Sitter applicants | 25+ | Potential caregiver supply |
| Shortlisted sitters | 10+ | Usable, qualified supply |
| Interested societies | 3–5 | Local demand concentration and B2B potential |

1. Paid Bookings — Target: 5–10

What this metric means

By Day 15, PetSaathi should have received payment for at least five genuine trial bookings.

A paid booking should be counted only when:

The customer has selected a service.

The customer lives inside the supported area.

The pet’s basic requirements have been reviewed.

A date or service period has been selected.

The price and conditions have been accepted.

The required payment or deposit has been received.

The payment is connected to a booking ID.

A verbal promise to pay later should not be counted as a paid booking.

Similarly, a payment link that was opened but not completed should not be counted.

Why it is the most important metric

Payment shows that the customer values the service enough to make a financial commitment.

A survey response, website visit or positive comment shows interest. A payment shows purchase intent.

Target interpretation

### Table 84

| Actual result | Interpretation |
| --- | --- |
| 10 or more paid bookings | Strong initial payment validation |
| 5–9 paid bookings | Minimum target achieved |
| 3–4 paid bookings | Promising but incomplete evidence |
| 1–2 paid bookings | Weak payment validation |
| 0 paid bookings | Do not move directly into full MVP development |

Additional quality check

The bookings should not all come from close friends, relatives or team members.

At least some payments should come from genuine target customers who did not feel personally obligated to support the founder.

Questions to answer

Which service generated the payments?

Which area generated the payments?

Which price converted?

How many paid bookings were completed?

How many customers requested another booking?

Did the booking generate a positive contribution after sitter and operating costs?

Were there cancellations, complaints or refunds?

Simple explanation

“Paid bookings prove that customers are willing to exchange real money for PetSaathi’s service.”

2. Booking-Ready Leads — Target: 20 or More

What this metric means

A booking-ready lead is a customer who is close to making a real booking.

The person should meet most of the following conditions:

Lives in a selected launch area

Owns a suitable pet

Needs dog walking, pet sitting or boarding

Requires the service within approximately 30 days

Accepts the expected price range

Can provide a preferred date or time

Is willing to review a sitter match

Has provided permission for follow-up

Is prepared to pay when availability is confirmed

Lead scoring can combine customer characteristics with customer actions. HubSpot’s current lead-scoring tools similarly allow contacts to be prioritised using record properties, engagement activities or a combination of both.

Suggested booking-readiness score

### Table 85

| Condition | Points |
| --- | --- |
| Lives in the pilot area | 15 |
| Needs service within 30 days | 20 |
| Selects a specific service | 10 |
| Accepts the price range | 15 |
| Provides a preferred date | 10 |
| Has previously paid for pet care | 10 |
| Wants to review or meet a sitter | 5 |
| Agrees to a paid trial | 10 |
| Allows follow-up | 5 |
| Total | 100 |

Classification

70–100 points: Booking-ready

40–69 points: Interested but not ready

Below 40 points: Early-stage or unsuitable lead

Why 20 leads are useful

Twenty booking-ready leads create a small but meaningful sales pipeline.

Not every qualified customer will immediately pay because:

Their required date may be later.

A suitable sitter may not be available.

They may want a meet-and-greet.

They may need family approval.

Their travel plan may not yet be confirmed.

However, twenty qualified leads suggest that additional bookings may be possible without restarting customer research from zero.

Simple explanation

“Booking-ready leads are suitable customers who need the service, accept the approximate price and are prepared to take the next booking step.”

3. Interested Pet Parents — Target: 50 or More

What this metric means

An interested pet parent should have performed a meaningful action.

Examples include:

Completing the customer-interest form

Requesting service details

Asking about the price

Selecting a service

Requesting a sitter profile

Joining the pilot list

Sharing a preferred area

Giving permission for follow-up

The following actions alone should not qualify someone as an interested lead:

Liking a post

Viewing an Instagram story

Visiting the website

Forwarding a message

Saying, “This is a good idea”

Following the social-media profile

Why this metric matters

This metric measures the wider demand pool.

A large interested group is useful because some customers may not need the service immediately but may require it during:

Travel

Office schedule changes

Festivals

Family events

Emergencies

Their regular caregiver’s absence

Interest-to-booking-readiness calculation

Formula:

Booking-ready leads ÷ Interested pet parents × 100

Example

If PetSaathi has 50 interested pet parents and 20 booking-ready leads:

20 ÷ 50 × 100 = 40%

This means that 40% of the interested group has developed into a near-term opportunity.

Warning sign

If PetSaathi has more than 50 interested customers but fewer than five booking-ready leads, the problem may be:

Weak qualification

Unclear pricing

Insufficient trust

Wrong service timing

Unsupported areas

Curiosity without serious demand

Simple explanation

“Interested pet parents show that the market recognises the service, but only booking-ready and paid customers demonstrate stronger demand.”

4. Sitter Applicants — Target: 25 or More

What this metric means

PetSaathi should receive at least 25 completed sitter applications.

An applicant should be counted only after submitting the required information.

The following should not be counted as completed applications:

Recruitment-post likes

People who ask one question

People who receive the application link but do not open it

Incomplete or duplicate submissions

Applicants outside the supported city with no realistic service fit

Why applicant volume matters

PetSaathi needs a reasonable pool because some applicants will not progress.

Applicants may be removed because of:

Location mismatch

Availability mismatch

Insufficient experience

Unsafe judgement

Refusal to complete verification

Unrealistic payout expectations

Poor communication

Failed reference checks

Boarding-home problems

Twenty-five applicants may therefore produce approximately ten serious shortlisted profiles.

What should be measured

### Table 86

| Sitter metric | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| Application completion rate | Recruitment-form effectiveness |
| Area distribution | Whether supply matches demand |
| Service distribution | Walking, sitting or boarding availability |
| Availability distribution | Morning, evening and weekend coverage |
| Expected payout | Supply economics |
| Verification readiness | Screening feasibility |
| Interview attendance | Reliability |
| Shortlist rate | Applicant quality |

Simple explanation

“Sitter applications measure potential supply, but application volume alone does not prove that suitable caregivers are available.”

5. Shortlisted Sitters — Target: 10 or More

What this metric means

A shortlisted sitter is an applicant who has passed the initial evaluation and is suitable for further verification, training or trial bookings.

The sitter should generally:

Live near the pilot areas

Have useful availability

Demonstrate relevant pet-care experience

Communicate professionally

Show safe judgement

Accept service-reporting requirements

Agree to identity and address checks

Match the expected payout model

Attend screening calls or interviews reliably

Be suitable for at least one specific service

Shortlisting does not mean full approval

The following stages should remain separate:

Application received

Initial screening completed

Shortlisted

Documents reviewed

Interview completed

Training completed

Service assessment completed

Approved for specific services

A sitter should not be presented publicly as fully verified simply because the application was shortlisted.

Why ten shortlisted sitters are valuable

Ten suitable sitters may provide:

Better time-slot coverage

Backup support

Different service capabilities

Reduced cancellation risk

Better geographical coverage

The ability to match pet size and behaviour with sitter experience

However, PetSaathi must check whether these ten sitters are located where customer demand exists.

Ten sitters spread across distant areas may be less useful than six strong sitters concentrated around one launch zone.

Supply-demand matching table

### Table 87

| Area | Booking-ready leads | Shortlisted sitters | Decision |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Area A | 12 | 6 | Strong match |
| Area B | 6 | 3 | Promising |
| Area C | 4 | 1 | Supply gap |

Simple explanation

“Shortlisted sitters represent the usable caregiver supply that may support real bookings after final checks and training.”

6. Interested Societies — Target: 3–5

What this metric means

An interested society should have agreed to a specific next action.

Examples include:

Sharing the PetSaathi survey

Arranging a committee meeting

Allowing a presentation

Reviewing a pilot proposal

Discussing a pet-care camp

Introducing PetSaathi to pet-owning residents

Collecting voluntary registrations

A society should not be counted merely because the manager:

Accepted a brochure

Read the message

Said, “We will see”

Provided a general email address

Allowed one informal conversation

Why societies matter

Society partnerships may create customer density.

Several customers in the same society can provide:

Shorter sitter travel time

Lower customer-acquisition costs

Easier service scheduling

Stronger local trust

More referrals

Better prospects for repeat walking plans

Interested-society quality check

For each interested society, record:

Number of flats

Estimated pet-owning households

Authorised contact person

Permission status

Proposed activity

Meeting date

Resident-survey status

Current service demand

Nearby sitter availability

Next decision date

Warning sign

Three interested societies are not useful when:

They are located far from each other.

No sitter can serve them.

The managers have no authority.

Residents have not expressed demand.

Local rules prevent the proposed activity.

Simple explanation

“Interested societies demonstrate the possibility of building concentrated demand through apartment communities.”

Before Making the Final Decision

Step 1: Verify the Data

Remove or correct:

Duplicate leads

Test submissions

Internal team payments

Cancelled payment tests

Incomplete sitter applications

Unsupported service areas

Incorrect lead sources

Unconfirmed society interest

Step 2: Confirm Important Analytics Events

GA4 allows actions that are particularly important to the business to be marked as key events. PetSaathi may use confirmed form submissions, payment completions and trial bookings as important measurement events.

Website analytics should support the CRM, but the CRM and confirmed payment records should remain the primary sources for the Day 15 decision.

Step 3: Review Service Delivery

Ask:

Were the paid services completed successfully?

Did sitters arrive on time?

Were report cards submitted?

Did customers receive updates?

Were any refunds required?

Were any safety incidents reported?

Did customers request the same sitter?

Did customers show repeat-booking interest?

Step 4: Review Unit Economics

For every completed service, calculate:

Customer payment − sitter payout − payment cost − support cost − travel subsidy − refund cost

A service does not need to be highly profitable during the pilot, but the future regular price should provide a plausible path to positive contribution.

Step 5: Check Area Density

Identify whether customer demand, sitter supply and partner support overlap in one or two micro-markets.

Hard Gates and Soft Targets

PetSaathi should not make the decision by simply adding all six numbers.

Some conditions are hard gates, while others are supporting evidence.

Hard gates

The following conditions should normally be required before Phase 2:

At least five genuine paid bookings

Enough suitable sitters to fulfil the next batch

No unresolved critical safety problem

A repeatable manual booking process

Clear cancellation and emergency procedures

At least one area with customer and sitter density

A plausible pricing and payout model

No known legal or operational blocker for the selected service

Soft targets

The following targets strengthen the decision but may not individually block progression:

Fifty interested pet parents

Twenty booking-ready leads

Twenty-five sitter applications

Ten shortlisted sitters

Three to five interested societies

For example, PetSaathi may have only two interested societies but still possess strong direct customer demand and eight paid bookings.

In that case, the society channel may remain a secondary experiment rather than preventing the complete project from moving forward.

Final Decision Framework

Decision 1: GO — Move to Phase 2

PetSaathi should move to Phase 2 when:

Five to ten paid bookings have been received.

Several paid services have been completed successfully.

Twenty or more customers are booking-ready.

A clear primary service has emerged.

One launch area shows meaningful density.

Suitable sitters are available in that area.

Customers understand and trust the service process.

Pricing can reasonably support sitter and operational costs.

No serious safety or compliance blocker remains.

What Phase 2 should include

Phase 2 should build only the validated core.

For example:

Primary launch area: Vesu

Primary service: Dog walking

Secondary service: Pet sitting

First MVP modules: Profiles, booking, availability, payments and report cards

Boarding: Remain manual or delayed until compliance and supply are stronger

Stripe’s startup guidance recommends using an MVP as the simplest version that solves the customer problem, allowing the business to test the market without making an unnecessarily heavy initial investment.

Decision 2: CONDITIONAL GO — Extend Validation

A conditional go is appropriate when the evidence is promising but incomplete.

Example conditions include:

Three or four paid bookings

Twelve to nineteen booking-ready leads

Six to nine shortlisted sitters

Strong interest but insufficient booking dates

One promising society relationship

Payment friction that can be corrected

Demand concentrated in only one service

Recommended action

Extend Phase 1 for another 10–15 days.

Do not begin full marketplace development.

During the extension:

Focus on the strongest area.

Offer only the strongest service.

Resolve the main trust objection.

Recruit sitters for the missing time slots.

Retest the best price.

Complete additional paid services.

Measure repeat bookings.

Decision 3: NO-GO FOR PHASE 2 — Pause and Redesign

PetSaathi should not begin major MVP development when:

Zero to two genuine paid bookings were received.

Customers express interest but consistently refuse payment.

No area shows demand concentration.

Suitable sitters cannot be recruited.

Customer prices cannot support sitter payouts.

Serious safety failures occurred.

The booking process cannot be completed reliably.

Boarding or another service faces unresolved regulatory issues.

Most demand comes from friends rather than target customers.

Customers do not request repeat use.

A no-go decision does not necessarily mean permanently ending PetSaathi.

It means the current assumptions have not been validated.

The team may need to change:

The customer segment

The launch area

The first service

The pricing

The trust process

The sitter model

The acquisition channel

Traffic-Light Decision Table

### Table 88

| Metric | Green | Amber | Red |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Paid bookings | 5–10+ | 3–4 | 0–2 |
| Booking-ready leads | 20+ | 12–19 | Below 12 |
| Interested pet parents | 50+ | 30–49 | Below 30 |
| Sitter applicants | 25+ | 15–24 | Below 15 |
| Shortlisted sitters | 10+ | 6–9 | Below 6 |
| Interested societies | 3–5+ | 1–2 | 0 |

Decision interpretation

Mostly green with all hard gates passed

Decision: GO

Move to a focused Phase 2 MVP.

Combination of green and amber

Decision: CONDITIONAL GO

Extend validation and solve the identified weakness.

Paid bookings or safety in red

Decision: NO-GO FOR PHASE 2
